The quantum group structure of long-range integrable deformations arXiv:
Koen Schouten, and Marius de Leeuw

Abstract
Quantum integrable spin chains are known to possess a large family of long-range deformations generated by the local, boost and bilocal operators. Although these deformations are well-understood on the level of the pairwise commuting charges, the underlying quantum group structures had not yet been recognised. In this paper, we provide a quantum group-theoretical description for the family of long-range deformations of arbitrary homogeneous Yang-Baxter integrable spin chains up to first order in the deformation parameter. In particular, we show that the deformations are obtained via a twist of the algebraic structure of the underlying quantum group. This twisting results in a generally non-associative algebra that has a non-trivial Drinfeld associator. The Drinfeld associator is then shown to encode the information about the long-range interaction terms for the integrable spin chain. Moreover, the deformed quantum group is shown to contain a large perturbatively associative substructure, thus ensuring the perturbative integrability of the long-range model. The deformed quantum group provides explicit expressions for the Lax operators and R R -matrices of the long-range deformed models, which manifestly satisfy the RLL relation and the Yang-Baxter equation up to first order in the deformation parameter. In order to derive the results, we introduce algebra elements that we call the algebraic charge densities. As a side result, we provide a conjecture for the explicit expressions of the undeformed charge densities in terms of these algebra elements.



1
Introduction

Quantum integrable models, characterised by the existence of a “sufficiently large” set of independent mutually-commuting conserved charges, have been of both mathematical and physical interest for nearly a century. The knowledge of such a set of pairwise commuting charges allows one to decompose the Hilbert space into smaller sectors, thus reducing the problem of the spectral decomposition into simpler components. This was first utilised by Bethe in 1931 to find the exact solutions of the Heisenberg spin chain, using what is now called the
Bethe Ansatz

19
. One of the main ideas behind the Bethe Ansatz, and therefore quantum integrability, is that the elastic many-body scattering factorises into a product of two-body scatterings
21
. In this way, all the physical excitations can be described in terms of the two-body scattering states. For this factorisation to be consistent, the scattering matrix is required to satisfy the
Yang-Baxter equation

98
;
6
;
95
. Quantum integrable models could therefore be (partially) classified by the solutions, called the
R
-matrices
, of the Yang-Baxter equation
65
;
93
;
59
;
97
;
52
;
35
;
83
;
27
;
26
;
25
;
44
;
67
. Moreover, the Yang-Baxter equation naturally gave rise to the development by the Leningrad-St. Petersburg school of an algebraic framework to describe quantum integrability, known as the
quantum inverse scattering method

89
;
95
;
43
;
90
;
39
;
91
;
64
;
92
;
62
(see
40
for an overview). In particular, using the
R
-matrix and a corresponding
Lax operator
, one can construct a generating function for the mutually commuting charges, and additionally derive their eigenstates and spectra using the
Algebraic Bethe Ansatz

42
;
41
.

The realisations of the Yang-Baxter equation led to new algebraic objects that could be viewed as quantisations of the classical Lie groups and Lie algebras, known as
quantum groups

91
;
64
. Drinfeld and Jimbo independently formalised the description of these quantum groups in the language of Hopf algebras (see e.g.
22
;
73
;
58
;
61
), which can be considered as one-parameter deformations of universal enveloping algebras of complex semisimple Lie groups
31
;
32
;
57
. The
universal
R
-matrix
for a (quasi-triangular) quantum group is then defined through the braiding properties of the coproduct structure, and satisfies the algebraic Yang-Baxter equation if the coproduct is
coassociative
. In this way, quantum integrable models can be obtained through specific representations of the quantum groups. For example, the Heisenberg spin chain corresponds to a representation of the
Yangian algebra

31
;
32
(see
71
;
80
;
18
for introductions). Instead of quantisations of the universal enveloping algebras, Faddeev, Reshetikhin and Takhtajan, following the spirit of non-commutative geometry
24
, later considered the quantisation of the function algebra over a Lie group, known as the
FRT-bialgebra

37
;
38
. These FRT-bialgebras can be viewed as the
dual objects
to the quantised universal enveloping algebra, and therefore describe the same underlying quantum group structure
61
. The commuting charges, as well as the
creation operators
for the eigenstates, are then realised as representations of elements of the FRT-bialgebra
33
. In particular, the FRT-bialgebras describe the full quantum group structure behind the quantum inverse scattering method.

Integrable structures have also been discovered to be present in the AdS/CFT correspondence
77
. In particular, in the planar limit
1
of the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM model, it was shown that the dilatation operator acting on single trace primary operators can be identified with an integrable nearest-neighbour spin chain Hamiltonian at one-loop order
78
. Integrability techniques could therefore be used to compute the scaling dimensions of primary local operators at one-loop order
14
;
15
. At higher-loop corrections, the dilatation operator was shown to be given by an integrable spin chain Hamiltonian involving higher-range interactions at each perturbation order of the ‘t Hooft coupling
9
;
7
;
16
(see
84
for a review). This finding later led to a more general class of
long-range deformations
of integrable spin chains, in which the range of interactions grows linearly with the perturbation order
8
;
4
;
5
. In particular, by deforming the integrable charges using a
deformation equation
, one obtains a new set of perturbatively integrable long-range charges. A family of such long-range deformations was found to be generated by the
local
,
boost
, and
bilocal
operators
4
;
5
. Other known long-range integrable spin chains, such as the Haldane-Shastry
49
;
87
and the Inozemtsev spin chain
56
;
55
, also fall under this family of long-range models. Since there is a large tower of mutually commuting charges, it is also expected that one could derive the eigenvalues and eigenstates for the charges using the Bethe Ansatz. In the thermodynamic limit, i.e. on the doubly-infinite spin chain, this is indeed known to be possible using the
Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz

47
;
2
;
20
;
3
. However, on finite-size spin chains, it has remained unknown how to perform a Bethe Ansatz for which
wrapping corrections
are included, i.e. when the interaction range exceeds the length of the spin chain. Similarly, up until now, it was not known whether these long-range deformed models have a corresponding underlying quantum group structure.

In this paper, we provide, for the first time, a quantum group-theoretical description for the long-range deformations of arbitrary homogeneous Yang-Baxter integrable spin chains up to first order in the deformation parameter. Since the long-range integrable models correspond to deformations of the commuting charges, it is also expected that these are obtained through deformations of the underlying quantum group. Some progress in understanding this quantum group structure was already made in
45
;
46
;
68
, where some general properties for the long-range Lax operators were described. In this paper, we will improve on these results by explicitly describing the corresponding deformations of the FRT-bialgebra up to first order in the deformation parameter, thus providing a concrete foundation for the understanding of the quantum group structure. In particular, the long-range deformed quantum groups are shown to be obtained by means of
twisting
, which deforms the multiplication such that the quantum group becomes
non-associative
and acquires a non-trivial
Drinfeld associator

34
. This breaking of the associativity is necessary for the long-range interactions to appear. Namely, for associative quantum groups, the
R
-matrix factorises into range-two terms, such that the associated integrable model only contains nearest-neighbour interactions. The non-associativity and the appearance of the non-trivial Drinfeld associator in the deformed quantum algebra, however, also allow for higher-range corrections. In particular, the Drinfeld associator exactly encodes the information about the long-range deformation. Moreover, in order to ensure the (perturbative) integrability of the long-range deformed model, the deformed quantum group retains a large perturbatively associative substructure up to first order in the deformation parameter. The long-range deformed quantum groups then provide explicit expressions for the Lax operators and
R
-matrices for the deformed models, which manifestly satisfy the RLL-relations and the Yang-Baxter equation due to this (perturbatively) associative substructure. This then also shows that the integrable long-range spin chains are Yang-Baxter integrable up to first order in the deformation parameter.

In order to describe the deformations, we introduce specific algebra elements, which we call the
algebraic charge densities
and which are obtained through derivations of the universal
R
-matrix. Similar to the
R
-matrix, these algebraic charge densities have particularly nice algebraic properties, and, as a side result, are conjectured to give explicit expressions for the charge densities of the undeformed integrable spin chains. We remark that these results are completely general and may be applied to any homogeneous Yang-Baxter integrable spin chain. Moreover, the algebraic charge densities are shown to provide new techniques for deriving the
generalised Sutherland equations
, and also for proving the Yang-Baxter integrability of the long-range deformations.

General overview

Let us provide a general overview of the framework that is used to describe the long-range deformations in this paper. Throughout the paper, we will consider arbitrary
R
-matrices
\mathcal{R}(u,v)\in\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V)
that satisfy the Yang-Baxter equation

\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{3})=\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2})

(1)

and are regular, i.e.
\mathcal{R}(u,u)=\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{P}
the permutation operator. Recall here that in specific cases these
R
-matrices arise from representations of (pseudo)-quasitriangular quantised universal enveloping algebras, like the Yangian or quantum affine algebra. One could use this interpretation to define “coproducts” of the
R
-matrices, even if there is no clear underlying quantum universal enveloping algebra. For example, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{(12)3}(u_{1},u_{2};u_{3})&\coloneq(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{3})\\
\mathcal{R}_{1(23)}(u_{1};u_{2},u_{3})&\coloneq(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2}).\end{split}

(2)

More generally, one can consider
N-1
consecutive coproducts on the first leg of the
R
-matrix, and
M-N-1
coproducts on the second leg, to get

\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M})\coloneq\overset{N}{\underset{i=1}{\overrightarrow{\prod}}}\overset{M}{\underset{\ j=N+1}{\overleftarrow{\prod}}}\mathcal{R}_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j}),

(3)

for
0<N<M
. Formally, as we will describe in Section
2.3
, these “coproducts” correspond to specific evaluations of the
universal
r
-form
of the associated FRT-bialgebra. One can now also consider the associated Lax operator
\mathscr{L}_{a1}(u)\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{a1}(u,0)
and monodromy matrix
\mathcal{T}_{a}(u)\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{aL}(u)\cdots\mathscr{L}_{a1}(u)
, which correspond to specific representations of the associated FRT-bialgebra. The transfer-matrix
t(u)\coloneq\mathrm{tr}_{a}\mathcal{T}_{a}(u)
is then well-known to generate a tower of mutually-commuting local charges given by

\mathbb{Q}_{k}\coloneq\frac{d^{k-2}}{du^{k-2}}\left(t(u)^{-1}\frac{d}{du}t(u)\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{L}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1},

(4)

for
k\geq 2
and where
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
are the corresponding charge densities.

The central objects throughout this paper are the
algebraic charge densities
, which correspond to derivatives of the
R
-matrices. In particular, let us define a derivative of the
R
-matrix on the spectral parameters corresponding to the first leg:

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M})&\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{d}{du_{i}}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M}).\end{split}

(5)

For
k\geq 2
, the algebraic charge densities are then defined to be given by

\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}\coloneq\mathcal{D}_{1}^{k-2}\left(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}\right),

(6)

where we have left out the explicit dependence on the spectral parameter in the notation of the above equation. Remark here the similarity with (
4
). In Section
2.5
, we provide a more formal definition in terms of bilinear maps on the FRT-bialgebra. As examples, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}(u,v)\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v),\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{R}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{12}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v),\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w).

One may now recognise that the second algebraic charge density evaluated at zero spectral parameters is exactly equal to the charge density for the second charge, i.e.
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12}=\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(0,0)
. Similarly, as we show in Section
2.5
, the third charge density is given by a difference of algebraic charge densities, that is
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{123}=\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}(0,0,0)-\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{23}(0,0)
. In particular, this leads us to conjecture that all the charge densities are given by

\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}(0,...,0)-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}(0,...,0).

(8)

This then provides a closed formula for all the charge densities of arbitrary Yang-Baxter integrable spin chains in terms of the
R
-matrix and derivations thereof. The algebraic charge densities also provide a generalised formula for the Sutherland equation. In particular, let
\mathscr{L}_{a(1,...,k)}=\mathscr{L}_{ak}\cdots\mathscr{L}_{a1}
. As we show in Section
2.6
, the Sutherland equation for the
k
-th charge density is given by

[\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k},\mathscr{L}_{a(1,...,k)}]=\mathscr{L}_{a(2,...,k)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,a,(2,...,k-1)}\mathscr{L}_{a1}-\mathscr{L}_{a(3,...,k)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2,a,(3,...,k)}\mathscr{L}_{a(12)},

(9)

where we introduce

{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,2,(3,...,k)}(u)\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(2,...,k)}(0;u,0,...,0)-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}(0,u)\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(3,...,k)}(0,...,0)\mathcal{R}_{12}(0,u)

(10)

as the
reduced algebraic charge densities
as defined in Section
2.5
. Remark that
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,2,(3,...,k)}(0)=\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}
since
\mathcal{R}_{12}(0,0)=\mathcal{P}_{12}
.

These generalised Sutherland equations now enable us to provide explicit expressions for the Lax operators and
R
-matrices corresponding to the long-range deformations up to first order in the deformation parameter. In particular, consider the monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain
\mathscr{L}_{a,(...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{a,m}
and on the half-infinite spin chain
\mathscr{L}_{a,(n,...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}}\mathscr{L}_{a,m}
and
\mathscr{L}_{a,(...,n)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq n}}\mathscr{L}_{a,m}
. As discussed in Section
3.2
, it follows from (
9
) that the commutator between the monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain and the boost operator
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}]\coloneq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1}
is given by

[\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}],\mathscr{L}_{a,(...)}]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot[\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1},\mathscr{L}_{a,(...)}]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{a(n+1,...)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{n,a,(n+1,...,n+k-2)}\mathscr{L}_{a,(...,n)},

(11)

for
k\geq 3
. Similarly, the Sutherland equation can be used to compute the commutator between the bilocal operators and the monodromy matrix, see Section
3.2
. It is known that these boost and bilocal operators generate long-range deformations of the spin chain
4
;
5
. It can be seen in (
11
) that the commutator with the boost operator is given by a sum over insertions of the reduced algebraic charge density at position
n
. As discussed in Section
3.2
, such a deformed monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain may also be obtained through the Lax operator

\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}(u)\coloneq\left(1+\lambda\cdot{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{n,a,(b_{1},...,b_{k-2})}(u)\right)\mathscr{L}_{(a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(12)

where
\mathscr{L}_{(a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}(u)=\mathscr{L}_{an}(u)\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},n}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{b_{k-2},n}
. Similarly, we also derive expressions for the Lax operators corresponding to the bilocal deformation in Section
3.2
. We therefore obtain general expressions for the Lax operators of the long-range deformations of arbitrary integrable spin chains. Moreover, as we discuss in Section
3.3
, these deformed Lax operators correspond to representations of
twisted
FRT-bialgebras. The explicit descriptions of these twisted algebras then also provide expressions for the corresponding
R
-matrices. For example, as derived in Section
3.3
, the
R
-matrix for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(12)(34)}(u,0;v,0)&\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{(12)(34)}(u,0;v,0)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\\
&\quad+\lambda\left({}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{4,1,2}(u)\mathcal{R}_{(12)(34)}(u,0;v,0)-\mathcal{R}_{(12)(34)}(u,0;v,0){}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{2,3,4}(v)\right),\end{split}

(13)

where
\mathcal{R}_{(12)(34)}(u,0;v,0)\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{14}(u,0)\mathcal{R}_{13}(u,v)\mathcal{R}_{24}(0,0)\mathcal{R}_{23}(0,v)
. It is shown in Section
3.3
that these Lax operators and
R
-matrices correspond to representations of an associative subalgebra of the twisted quantum algebra, which ensures that the RLL relation and the Yang-Baxter equation hold. We also provide
R
-matrices for the more general boost deformations and the bilocal deformations. One should remark here that, for the long-range deformations, the Lax operators and the
R
-matrices are generally not equal to one another. The origin of this difference may be traced back to the description of the underlying FRT-bialgebra, which is given by a twisted double-crossed product bialgebra as explained in Section
3.3
.

Outline

The outline of this paper is as follows:

•

In Section
2
, we provide a short review of FRT-bialgebras and discuss how they describe the associated spin chains. In particular, we begin our discussion by giving the definition of an
R
-matrix and the corresponding FRT-bialgebra in Section
2.1
. Then, in Section
2.2
, we discuss how these FRT-bialgebras can be interpreted as the dual of the more usual quantised universal enveloping algebras, like the Yangian. In Section
2.3
, we review the concept of coquasitriangularity of these FRT-bialgebras, and show how the Lax operators arise as representations induced from the
\ell
-functionals of the FRT-bialgebras. In Section
2.4
, we discuss the tower of commuting charges that are generated in the FRT-bialgebra. Then, in Section
2.5
, we will introduce the algebraic charge densities, which are shown to be related to the charge densities, and we derive some of their properties. In particular, we use the algebraic charge densities to give a conjecture for a general formula for the charge densities of the mutually commuting charges. Lastly, in Section
2.6
, we use the algebraic charge densities to derive the higher-order variants of the Sutherland equation.

•

In Section
3
, we will discuss the long-range deformations of integrable spin chains. In particular, we will begin in Section
3.1
by recalling the three families of long-range deformations as described in
4
;
5
. Namely, as we will review, the long-range deformations are generated by the local operators, boost operators and bilocal operators. In Section
3.2
, we then use the higher-order Sutherland equations to derive the Lax operators for the three families of long-range deformations up to first order in the deformation parameter. Afterwards, in Section
3.3
, we discuss the deformations of the FRT-bialgebra that correspond to the long-range deformations. In particular, we will give explicit twisting elements up to first order in the deformation parameter and show that the twisted Lax operators correspond to the long-range deformation, and that there exist
R
-matrices such that the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation are satisfied. In Section
3.4
, we then explicitly work out the examples for the boost
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
and bilocal
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformations of the XXX Heisenberg spin chain. Lastly, in Section
3.5
, we discuss the corresponding long-range deformation of the Yangian algebra.

•

In Appendix
A
, we will provide the proofs of the various statements that are made in the main text. Namely, the proofs are somewhat lengthy, and in order to maintain the readability of the main text, we therefore provide the proofs in the appendix.

•

In Appendix
B
, we will discuss the relation between the boost
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation of the XXX Heisenberg spin chain and the
\Theta
-morphism that was described in
48
. In particular, in that paper, the
\Theta
-morphism was used to explicitly derive the transfer matrix and the
N
-magnon creation operators for the long-range deformed XXX spin chain. We show that, in our framework, we obtain the same results. We then use this relation to identify the
N
-magnon creation operators within our deformed FRT-bialgebra, thus bringing us a small step closer to understanding the long-range Algebraic Bethe Ansatz.

Conventions.
In this paper, the tensor product
\otimes
will denote the ordinary tensor product over
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces or
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. For an algebra
\mathcal{A}
and an element
x\in\mathcal{A}
, we use the leg-numbering notation for its inclusion in an
n
-fold tensor product. In particular, we will write
x_{i}\coloneq 1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Similarly, for
x\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
, the notation
x_{ij}
denotes the element as considered in
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
in the
i
-th and
j
-th factor. This notation generalises to elements
x_{i_{1},...,i_{m}}
in the higher tensor product
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes m}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}
for
m\leq n
. We will denote by
\mathbb{C}[\![u]\!]
the formal power series over
\mathbb{C}
, and by
\mathbb{C}(\!(u)\!)
the Laurent series over
\mathbb{C}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{A}[\![u]\!]\coloneq\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\![u]\!]
and
\mathcal{A}(\!(u)\!)\coloneq\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}(\!(u)\!)
denote the formal power series and the Laurent series with coefficients in the algebra
\mathcal{A}
, respectively. We remark that for most of this paper, algebras will be considered to be unital and associative. However, in Section
3.3
, we will also encounter non-associative algebras. It will be explicitly stated when such non-associative algebras are considered.

2
Review of FRT-bialgebras

We will begin our discussion with a review of FRT-bialgebras and the associated integrable spin chains, which is mostly meant to introduce the basic notation and concepts necessary for the description of the long-range deformed algebras that will be discussed in Section
3
. The FRT-bialgebras, introduced by Faddeev, Reshetikhin and Takhtajan, can be naturally viewed as deformations/quantisations of the algebra of functions on an (affine) Lie group/algebra
37
;
38
, and are defined through the solutions to the Yang-Baxter equation. These FRT-bialgebras can be considered as the dual description of the usual Drinfeld-Jimbo approach to quantum groups, which are quantisations of the corresponding (affine) Lie algebras
31
;
32
;
57
. Moreover, the FRT-bialgebras describe the underlying algebraic structure behind the quantum inverse scattering method
89
;
95
;
43
;
90
;
39
;
91
;
64
;
92
;
62
;
40
. In Section
2.1
, we will review the definition of the
R
-matrix and the associated FRT-bialgebra. Then, in Section
2.2
, we will discuss how one can interpret these FRT-bialgebras as the algebra of functions over the corresponding Drinfeld-Jimbo type quantum groups. In Section
2.3
, we then review the concept of coquasitriangularity and
\ell
-functionals. In Section
2.4
, we review the tower of commuting charges that can be obtained from the representations of the FRT-bialgebra, thus constructing integrable spin chains. Then, in Section
2.5
, we introduce new algebra elements, the algebraic charge densities, which are conjectured to be related to the charge densities. Lastly, in Section
2.6
, we use the algebraic charge densities to derive the higher-order Sutherland equations, corresponding to the higher charge densities.

2.1
The FRT-bialgebra

In defining the FRT-bialgebras, we will closely follow the discussions as given in, for example,
36
;
82
(see also
73
). Before discussing the FRT-bialgebra, we will first introduce some notation. Throughout the paper, we let
V
be a finite-dimensional (complex) vector space. Moreover, we consider an element
\mathcal{R}(u,v)\in\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V)[\![u,v]\!]
, called the
R
-matrix
, that is analytic (and non-constant) in
u
and
v
and satisfies the
Yang-Baxter equation

98
;
6

\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{3})=\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1},u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2}),

(14)

for all
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, we will assume that the
R
-matrix is
regular
, i.e.
\mathcal{R}(u,u)=\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathcal{P}\colon v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v
is the permutation operator on
V\otimes V
, and that it is
unitary
, i.e. it satisfies the braiding unitarity equation
\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)\mathcal{R}_{21}(v,u)=I_{V\otimes V}
.
1
1

1

Remark here that the regularity of the
R
-matrix, together with the Yang-Baxter equation, already imply braiding unitarity. Indeed, for
u_{3}=u_{1}
, the Yang-Baxter equation (
14
) implies that
\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{21}(u_{2},u_{1})=\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2},u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{32}(u_{1},u_{2})
, which can only hold if
\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1},u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{21}(u_{2},u_{1})=\rho(u,v)I_{V\otimes V}
for some function
\rho(u,v)
. We then always assume the
R
-matrix to be normalised such that
\rho(u,v)=1
. Also, note that braiding unitarity generally only holds up to a discrete set of points in
\mathbb{C}
, as there might be specific points at which the
R
-matrix becomes singular.
The variables
u
and
v
will be referred to as the
spectral parameters
. Various classifications of solutions to the Yang-Baxter equation can be found in
65
;
93
;
59
;
97
;
52
;
35
;
83
;
27
;
26
;
25
;
44
;
67
.

We will fix an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
for
V
, where
N=\dim V
, and additionally consider the corresponding standard basis
\{E_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{N}
for
\mathrm{End}(V)
. Next, we let
\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle
be the free algebra generated by the elements
t^{(-k)}_{ij}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
1\leq i,j\leq N
. We define the corresponding (formal) generating function for the algebra elements to be given by
82

t_{ij}(u)\coloneq\delta_{ij}+t_{ij}^{(-1)}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}t^{(-k-1)}_{ij}u^{k}\in\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle[\![u]\!].

(15)

In order to lighten up the notation in the rest of this paper, we introduce the
N\times N
matrix
\mathcal{T}(u)\coloneq(t_{ij}(u))_{i,j=1}^{N}
with matrix coefficients
t_{ij}(u)
. Alternatively, one may use a similar notation as given in e.g.
79
;
80
and write

\mathcal{T}(u)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}E_{ij}\otimes t_{ij}(u)\in\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle t_{ij}^{(-k)}\rangle[\![u]\!].

(16)

This matrix may be interpreted as defining the
corepresentation

\varphi_{u}\colon V\to V\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle[\![u]\!]
given by
\varphi_{u}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}\otimes t_{ij}(u)
. The matrix
\mathcal{T}(u)
is then also called the
matrix corepresentation
of
\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle
. Of course, this is only a corepresentation if
\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle
is equipped with the appropriate coalgebra structure
22
, which we provide in Definition
2.1
. Suppose now that we have some tensor product
V^{\otimes m}
for
m\geq 1
, then we define the matrix of algebra elements at position
n
in this tensor product to be given by

\mathcal{T}_{n}(u)\coloneq\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\left[I_{V}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes E_{ij}\otimes I_{V}^{\otimes(m-n)}\right]\otimes t_{ij}(u)\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes m})\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle t_{ij}^{(-k)}\rangle[\![u]\!]

(17)

for
1\leq n\leq m
. Alternatively, this may be interpreted as applying the corepresentation
\varphi_{u}
in the
n
-th coordinate of the tensor product
V^{\otimes m}
. Similarly, we define the tensor product of matrices to be given by

\mathcal{T}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}(v)\coloneq\sum_{i,j,k,\ell=1}^{n}E_{ij}E_{k\ell}\otimes\left[t_{ij}(u)\otimes t_{k\ell}(v)\right]\in\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle t_{ij}^{(-k)}\rangle[\![u]\!]\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle t_{ij}^{(-k)}\rangle[\![v]\!].

(18)

This tensor product corresponds to the successive application of the corepresentation, i.e.
(\varphi_{u}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\varphi_{v}
. Remark that
E_{ij}E_{k\ell}=\delta_{jk}E_{i\ell}
, after which one recognises the ordinary coproduct rule for
\mathbb{C}\langle t^{(-k)}_{ij}\rangle

61
(see also Definition
2.1
). Note here that, in principle, the definitions in (
17
) and (
18
) may be combined to consider tensor products of a more general form like
\mathcal{T}_{n}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{m}(v)
, which is then defined naturally by the successive application of the corepresentation at different coordinates.

Based on
37
;
38
(see also e.g.
36
;
82
), we have the following definition:

2.1 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{R}(u,v)\in\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V)[\![u,v]\!]
be a regular solution to the Yang-Baxter equation (
14
). The associated
FRT-bialgebra
, denoted by
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, is the unital and associative algebra generated by the elements
t_{ij}^{(-k)}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
1\leq i,j\leq N
, subject to the relations

\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)\mathcal{T}_{1}(u)\mathcal{T}_{2}(v)=\mathcal{T}_{2}(v)\mathcal{T}_{1}(u)\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v),

(19)

and with a bialgebra structure given by
2
2

2

Recall that the coproduct is a homomorphism
\Delta\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\otimes\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Here,
\Delta(\mathcal{T}(u))
is defined as taking the coproduct at each matrix element of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}(u)
. Similarly, for the counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{C}
, we define
\varepsilon(\mathcal{T}(u))
as taking the counit at each matrix element.

\Delta(\mathcal{T}(u))=\mathcal{T}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon(\mathcal{T}(u))=I_{V}.

(20)

We refer to
61
;
22
;
58
;
73
for some standard literature on bialgebras, Hopf algebras and quantum groups. From now on forward, we will denote by
\mathcal{T}(u)
the corresponding matrix in
\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})[\![u]\!]
, i.e. view
\mathcal{T}(u)
as a matrix corepresentation of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Remark here that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
is a bialgebra, but
not
a Hopf algebra. In particular, to define an antipode on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, the inverse of the matrix
\mathcal{T}(u)
needs to be well-defined, which is only true in a completion of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. If we let
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
be the formal completion of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
relative to the descending filtration defined by
\deg t^{(-k)}_{ij}=k
, then
\mathcal{T}(u)
is invertible in
\text{End}(V)\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
, such that
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
is a Hopf algebra with antipode
S(\mathcal{T}(u))=\mathcal{T}(u)^{-1}

82
.

Lastly, we remark that our discussed FRT-bialgebras are not necessarily the same as the usual RTT-realisations of some known quantum groups. For example, for an
R
-matrix corresponding to the Yangian (see e.g.
31
;
80
;
79
), the associated FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
corresponds to the
dual Yangian

82
, which can be seen from the expansion in
u
of
t_{ij}(u)
as defined in (
15
), which is around
u=0
instead of around
u=\infty
. Therefore, one should not confuse the generators
t^{(-k)}_{ij}
for the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
with the generators for the Yangian. Moreover, for FRT-bialgebras related to deformations of specific classical Lie algebras, one often also imposes additional constraints on the algebra. For example, one might impose the quantum determinant to be equal to one, or impose additional orthogonality or symplectic conditions
80
. We will not impose such additional constraints to maintain generality. In the case of quantum affine algebras
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})
, it is usual to have a (trigonometric)
R
-matrix that depends on (multiplicative) spectral parameters
z_{1},z_{2}
such that
\mathcal{R}_{q}(z_{1},z_{2})=\mathcal{R}_{q}(z_{1}/z_{2})
(see e.g.
28
). However, under this parametrisation, the
R
-matrix is not analytic at
z_{1},z_{2}=0
, such that the above construction does not work. Therefore, one instead needs to consider the reparametrisation
z=q^{u}
, such that the
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}(u_{1},u_{2})\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{q}(q^{u_{1}-u_{2}})
is analytic around
u_{1},u_{2}=0
. Note that in that case, the corresponding FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
in Definition
2.1
differs from the usual constructions as given in, for example,
37
;
38
;
33
;
28
, as the algebra generators depend on the choice of parametrisation. In order to maintain generality in our discussion, we will always assume that
\mathcal{R}(u_{1},u_{2})
is analytic around
u_{1},u_{2}=0
, and therefore consider the appropriate corresponding parametrisations of the
R
-matrix and the “Yangian-like” FRT-bialgebras. However, all the techniques developed in this paper could, for example, also be directly applied to the “true” trigonometric FRT-bialgebra corresponding to the quantum affine algebra.

2.2
The dual description

It is well-known that the above-described FRT-algebraic construction for (affine) quantum groups corresponds to the dual description of the Drinfeld-Jimbo construction
33
, i.e. the quantisation of the universal enveloping algebras. In particular, there exists a dual pairing of bialgebras between them. Here, we will give a brief exposition of this duality, using the specific realisation of the Yangian as a simple example. Of course, the following generalises to more general quantum affine algebras.

Recall the definition of the Yangian: Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional complex simple Lie algebra and
\hbar>0
some deformation parameter. The Yangian
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
is the algebra generated by elements
x,\mathcal{J}(x)
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
, with
[x,\mathcal{J}(y)]=\mathcal{J}([x,y])
for
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
, together with the
Serre relations
(see e.g.
22
;
32
;
33
), which we will not denote explicitly. The Yangian may be viewed as a deformation of the universal enveloping algebra of the loop algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}[u])
. In particular, the Yangian
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
has a Hopf algebra structure given by

\displaystyle\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x,\quad

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathcal{J}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}(x)\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathcal{J}(x)+\frac{\hbar}{2}[x\otimes 1,t]

(21)

and
\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon(\mathcal{J}(x))=0
,
S(x)=-x
,
S(\mathcal{J}(x))=-\mathcal{J}(x)+\frac{1}{4}cx
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
. Here,
c
is the eigenvalue of the Casimir element
t
in the adjoint representation, which we regard as an element of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes 2}

22
. Moreover, there exists an automorphism
\mathcal{B}_{u}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
given by
\mathcal{B}_{u}(x)=x
and
\mathcal{B}_{u}(\mathcal{J}(x))=\mathcal{J}(x)+ux
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
, which corresponds to a reparametrisation of the spectral parameter
u
.

It was shown in
31
that the Yangian is
pseudo-triangular
: there exists an element
\mathcal{R}(u)=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}_{k}\left(\frac{\hbar}{u}\right)^{k}
, called the
universal
R
-matrix
, with
\mathcal{R}_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
such that

\begin{gathered}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R}(u))=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u)\mathcal{R}_{23}(u),\quad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{R}(u))=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u)\mathcal{R}_{12}(u),\\
\mathcal{R}(u)(\mathcal{B}_{u}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta(x))=(\mathcal{B}_{u}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}(x))\mathcal{R}(u),\end{gathered}

(22)

for all
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}=\sigma\circ\Delta
with
\sigma
the flip operator
\sigma\colon x\otimes y\mapsto y\otimes x
. Moreover, the
R
-matrix is
unitary
, i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{12}(u)\mathcal{R}_{21}(-u)=1\otimes 1
and it satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{u}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{v})\mathcal{R}(w)=\mathcal{R}(w+u-v)
. In the following, we will often use the (abuse of) notation
\mathcal{R}\coloneq\mathcal{R}(0)
.
3
3

3

Although
\mathcal{R}(0)
is not well-defined as an algebraic element in
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, it does have a well-defined expression in specific representations of the Yangian.
It follows immediately from (
22
) (see e.g.
31
;
33
) that the
R
-matrix satisfies the (now algebraic) Yang-Baxter equation

\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1}-u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1}-u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2}-u_{3})=\mathcal{R}_{23}(u_{2}-u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{13}(u_{1}-u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{12}(u_{1}-u_{2}),

(23)

for
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
. Note here that this
R
-matrix is of difference form, i.e. satisfies
\mathcal{R}(u,v)=\mathcal{R}(u-v)
.

Any representation of the Yangian algebra will now give rise to a matrix solution of the Yang-Baxter equation (
14
). Therefore, let
(\rho,V)
be a (finite-dimensional) representation of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
(\rho\otimes\rho)(\mathcal{R}(0))=\mathcal{P}
and define
\rho_{u}\coloneq\rho\circ\mathcal{B}_{u}
, which we call an
evaluation representation
at
u
.
4
4

4

In practice, this representation
\rho
should correspond to a fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{g}
in order for the dual pairing to be possibly non-degenerate. For example, for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,
\rho
should correspond to a spin-1/2 representation. It can be noted here that if we instead choose
\rho
to correspond to, for example, a spin-1 representation, the associated FRT-bialgebra is a subalgebra of the bialgebra for the spin-1/2 representation. Namely, any matrix element for the spin-1 representation can be written as a sum of products of matrix elements for the spin-1/2 representation.
The
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{\rho}(u,v)\coloneq(\rho\otimes\rho)(\mathcal{R}(u-v))
is now a regular solution to the Yang-Baxter equation (
14
) and therefore defines an associated FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
. Importantly, there is a
dual pairing
as bialgebras between
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, given by the bilinear map
33

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\times\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{C},\quad\langle t_{ij}(u),x\rangle\coloneq\langle e_{i}|\rho_{u}(x)|e_{j}\rangle,

(24)

which satisfies

\displaystyle\langle a\otimes b,\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle ab,x\rangle,\quad

\displaystyle\langle 1_{\mathcal{A}},x\rangle

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{Y}}(x),

(25)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}(a),x\otimes y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle a,xy\rangle\quad

\displaystyle\langle a,1_{\mathcal{Y}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}(a),

for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
and
x,y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and where
\langle a\otimes b,x\otimes y\rangle=\langle a,x\rangle\langle b,y\rangle

61
. In particular, this shows that the elements
t_{ij}(u)
in the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
correspond to matrix elements for the representation
\rho
of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})

33
. Through the dual pairing (
24
), one can then view elements in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
as functions
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{C}
, and elements in
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
as functions
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\to\mathbb{C}
, i.e.
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})^{*}
, where
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})^{*}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
are the bialgebra duals
61
(see also Section
2.3
). From (
25
), it can then be remarked that the product on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
corresponds to the coproduct on
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and vice versa. This can also explicitly be seen by comparing (
19
) and (
22
), where the
R
-matrix defines a braiding relation on the product of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
, while it gives a braiding relation on the
co
product of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. Similarly, the unit of one bialgebra corresponds to the counit of the other. Lastly, if we extend the dual pairing to the completion
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
, it can be shown
61
that the dual pairing (
24
) implies that
\langle S_{\mathcal{A}}(a),x\rangle=\langle a,S_{\mathcal{Y}}(x)\rangle
, such that the antipodes of the two Hopf algebras also correspond to each other. Remark that this duality is equivalent to the usual construction of the dual Yangian as in, for example,
82
.

Of course, the above construction is more generally true for affine quantum algebras and extends beyond Yangians. Although for a general
R
-matrix and FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, it is not necessarily known what the corresponding Drinfeld-Jimbo type quantum algebra is, one can still interpret the FRT-bialgebra as being dual to one, and think of the elements
t_{ij}(u)
as corresponding to matrix elements of a given (evaluation) representation. In the rest of this paper, we will solely work in terms of the FRT-bialgebras, while frequently coming back to this interpretation. Note here that in general, the dual pairing (
24
) is not non-degenerate. In order to obtain a non-degenerate pairing, one would need to quotient out by some ideal, which corresponds to imposing additional constraints on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. For example, one may require the quantum determinant to be equal to one, or impose additional orthogonality or symplectic conditions
33
. Again, we will not consider such quotients to maintain generality.

2.3
Coquasitriangularity and
\ell
-functionals

Since the Drinfeld-Jimbo construction of the quantum algebra admits a (pseudo) universal
R
-matrix, the duality as described in the previous section also implies the existence of a dual notion of the universal
R
-matrix for the FRT-bialgebra. In particular, the dual bracket (
24
) and the universal
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
induce a bilinear map
\mathbf{r}\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\to\mathbb{C}
, given by
\mathbf{r}(a,b)\coloneq\langle a\otimes b,\mathcal{R}\rangle
for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
. The function
\mathbf{r}
is then the dual object to the universal
R
-matrix, which we call the
universal
r
-form
. The properties that such a universal
r
-form satisfies are similar to the properties of the universal
R
-matrix, but where the roles of the comultiplication and multiplication are interchanged by the dual pairing through (
25
).

To state the dual notion of quasitriangularity for general bialgebras, for which we follow
61
, we introduce some notation. For a general bialgebra
\mathcal{A}
, we denote the coproduct for an element
a\in\mathcal{A}
by the Sweedler notation
\Delta(a)=\sum a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}
. Similarly, for a higher coproduct, one writes
\Delta^{(n)}(a)=\sum a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{(n+1)}
since the coproduct is coassociative. For linear maps
f,g\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
, we then define the convolution product
\ast
by
(f\ast g)(a)\coloneq\sum f(a_{(1)})g(a_{(2)})
for
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Similarly, one can define a “coproduct” on these functions by
\Delta(f)\colon\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C},(a,b)\mapsto f(ab)
for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
. Of course, this convolution product and coproduct on the space of functions just correspond to the ordinary product and coproduct in the dual algebra in the context of the dual pairing (
25
). Lastly, we define
\varepsilon(f)=f(1)
, after which the space of functions
\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
admits a bialgebra-like
5
5

5

Note here that, currently, the “coproduct” on the space of functions is a map
\Delta\colon\mathcal{A}^{*}\to(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})^{*}
. In order for
\mathcal{A}^{*}
to be a proper bialgebra, one requires the image of
\Delta
to lie in
\mathcal{A}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{*}\subset(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})^{*}
, which is not true in general for infinite-dimensional bialgebras. When we refer to the coproduct of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
, we will thus refer to this weaker notion of
\Delta
.
structure, which we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

We now have the following definition
61
;
58
:

2.2 Definition
.

A bialgebra
\mathcal{A}
is called
coquasitriangular
6
6

6

In literature, coquasitriangular bialgebras may also be called
dual quasitriangular bialgebras

76
,
braided bialgebras

66
;
29
, or
cobraided bialgebras

58
.
if there is a bilinear form
\mathbf{r}\colon\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
, called the
universal
r
-form
, such that:

•

\mathbf{r}
is invertible under the convolution product, i.e. there exists another bilinear form
\mathbf{r}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
(\mathbf{r}\ast\mathbf{r}^{-1})(a,b)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)=(\mathbf{r}^{-1}\ast\mathbf{r})(a,b)
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.

•

The universal
r
-form determines a braiding structure on the algebra, i.e.

\sum\mathbf{r}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})a_{(2)}b_{(2)}=\sum b_{(1)}a_{(1)}\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},b_{(2)}).

(26)

•

The universal
r
-form factorises under multiplication, i.e.

\begin{split}\mathbf{r}(a\cdot b,c)&=(\mathbf{r}_{13}\ast\mathbf{r}_{23})(a,b,c)=\sum\mathbf{r}(a,c_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(b,c_{(2)})\\
\mathbf{r}(a,b\cdot c)&=(\mathbf{r}_{13}\ast\mathbf{r}_{12})(a,b,c)=\sum\mathbf{r}(a_{(1)},c)\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},b),\end{split}

(27)

for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
, where we introduced the notation
\mathbf{r}_{12}(a,b,c)\coloneq\mathbf{r}(a,b)
, and analogously for
\mathbf{r}_{13}
and
\mathbf{r}_{23}
.

Moreover, the bialgebra
\mathcal{A}
is called
cotriangular
if
\mathbf{r}^{-1}=\mathbf{r}_{21}
, where
\mathbf{r}_{21}(a,b)\coloneq\mathbf{r}(b,a)
.

Any
r
-form can be shown to satisfy
\mathbf{r}(1,a)=\mathbf{r}(a,1)=\varepsilon(a)

61
. Also, one can recognise these properties to be the dual analogue of (
22
). It can be noted that the factorisation property (
27
) of the
r
-form corresponds with the associativity of the algebra. In particular, since
(ab)c=a(bc)
, the pictorially depicted braiding
(ab)c\to c(ab)
using the
r
-form in (
26
) can be factorised into the braiding of
bc\to cb
and
ac\to ca
. We emphasise here the necessity of associativity. In Section
3.3
, we will (weakly) break the associativity of the algebra, such that the factorisation property will not hold anymore in general. This breaking of the associative, and therefore non-factorisability, will be the main mechanism that gives rise to long-range deformations. Lastly, by combining
(
26
)
and
(
27
)
, it follows that the
r
-form satisfies the dual Yang-Baxter equation

\sum\mathbf{r}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},c_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(b_{(2)},c_{(2)})=\sum\mathbf{r}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(a_{(1)},c_{(2)})\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},b_{(2)}),

(28)

for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
.

Of course, any FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
is cotriangular by construction, where the universal
r
-form is simply given by
\mathbf{r}\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}(u),\mathcal{T}_{2}(v)\right)\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)
. Here, the cotriangularity (as opposed to coquasitriangularity) is a consequence of the braiding unitarity of the
R
-matrix. The evaluation of the
r
-form for products of elements in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
can then be obtained through the factorisation property (
27
) of the
r
-form, for which the Yang-Baxter equation (
14
) provides a consistency condition to the associativity of the underlying algebra. Here, a product of elements of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
in
\mathbf{r}
will correspond to the coproduct in the universal
R
-matrix in the dual picture, and we will use the notation
7
7

7

Here,
\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\coloneq a_{1}\cdots a_{N}
, while
\overleftarrow{\prod}_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\coloneq a_{N}\cdots a_{1}
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M})&\coloneq\mathbf{r}\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}(u_{1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}(u_{N}),\mathcal{T}_{N+1}(u_{N+1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}(u_{M})\right)\\
&=\overset{N}{\underset{i=1}{\overrightarrow{\prod}}}\overset{M}{\underset{\ j=N+1}{\overleftarrow{\prod}}}\mathcal{R}_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j}),\end{split}

(29)

for
0<N<M
. On the right, one can recognise the same formula as the coproduct of a universal
\mathcal{R}
-matrix of a quasitriangular bialgebra, where the brackets in the subscript of
\mathcal{R}_{(1,\cdots,N)(N+1,...,M)}
denote the
(N-1)
-th and
(M-N-1)
-th coproduct on the first and second coordinate of
\mathcal{R}
, respectively. In particular, in the dual picture for the universal
R
-matrix, we have
\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}\coloneq(\Delta^{N-1}\otimes\Delta^{M-N-1})(\mathcal{R})
.

Importantly, the universal
r
-form naturally induces a homomorphism from a bialgebra
\mathcal{A}
to its dual. Indeed, consider the following definition:

2.3 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a cotriangular bialgebra and
a\in\mathcal{A}
. The
\ell
-functional
is the map
\bm{\ell}(a)\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
given by
\bm{\ell}(a)\coloneq\mathbf{r}(a,\cdot)
.
8
8

8

Note that if
\mathcal{A}
is coquasitriangular, there also exists another
\ell
-functional
\bm{\ell}^{-}\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{-1}(\cdot,a)
, which coincides with
\bm{\ell}
in the cotriangular case. Also, we use a different convention from, for example
61
, where the element
a
is inserted in the second coordinate of
\mathbf{r}
. Instead, we use a convention which better aligns with most physics literature.

Now let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*}
be the bialgebra generated by these functionals
\bm{\ell}(a)
for
a\in\mathcal{A}
. In that case, the map
\bm{\ell}\colon a\mapsto\bm{\ell}(a)
is a bialgebra homomorphisms from
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cop}}
to
\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cop}}
is the coopposite bialgebra of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. the bialgebra with coproduct
\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}\coloneq\sigma\circ\Delta
, where
\sigma
the flip operator. Indeed, it follows from (
27
) that

\begin{split}\langle c,\bm{\ell}(ab)\rangle&=\sum\bm{\ell}(a)(c_{(1)})\bm{\ell}(b)(c_{(2)})=\langle\Delta(c),\bm{\ell}(a)\otimes\bm{\ell}(b)\rangle=\langle c,\bm{\ell}(a)\bm{\ell}(b)\rangle\\
\langle bc,\bm{\ell}(a)\rangle&=\sum\bm{\ell}(a_{(1)})(c)\bm{\ell}(a_{(2)})(b)=\langle b\otimes c,\bm{\ell}(\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}(a))\rangle=\langle b\otimes c,\Delta(\bm{\ell}(a))\rangle,\end{split}

(30)

for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
, where we used the notation
\langle b,\bm{\ell}(a)\rangle\coloneq\bm{\ell}(a)(b)=\mathbf{r}(a,b)
.

In the context of the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
, as described in Section
2.2
, under the dual bracket (
24
), the algebra
\mathcal{U}
can be identified with a subalgebra of a certain completion of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, which we denote by
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. Therefore, the function
\bm{\ell}(a)\coloneq(\langle a,\cdot\rangle\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R})
gives a bialgebra homomorphism

\bm{\ell}\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})^{\mathrm{cop}}\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}).

(31)

In particular, any representation of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
induces a representation of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})^{\mathrm{cop}}
through the
\ell
-functional. Of course, this could now, in principle, be a representation different from
\rho
. However, for our purposes, we will choose the representation to be the same. In that case, the elements
t_{ij}(u)\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
correspond to matrix elements of
\rho_{u}
, such that evaluation of
\bm{\ell}(a)
at these matrix elements
t_{ij}(u)
corresponds to the matrix elements of the matrix
\rho_{u}(\bm{\ell}(a))
in
\mathrm{End}(V)
. For a general FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, one defines the evaluation representation
(\rho_{u},V)
of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{\mathrm{cop}}
to be given by
\rho_{u}(a)\coloneq\bm{\ell}(a)(\mathcal{T}(u))\in\mathrm{End}(V)
for
a\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
.

By restricting to the elements in the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}(u)
, one can now define an operator
\mathscr{L}(u,v)\in\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)
, called the
Lax operator
, given by

\mathscr{L}_{a1}(u,v)\coloneq\rho_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{a}(u))=\bm{\ell}(\mathcal{T}_{a}(u))(\mathcal{T}_{1}(v))=\mathbf{r}(\mathcal{T}_{a}(u),\mathcal{T}_{1}(v))=\mathcal{R}_{a1}(u,v),

(32)

which just corresponds to the evaluation of the representation
\rho_{v}
at the matrix elements of
\mathcal{T}_{a}(u)
, where
a
denotes a coordinate on the
auxiliary space
of the matrix corepresentation, while
1
is a coordinate on the actual representation space
V
. Note here that the Lax operator therefore corresponds to a representation of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{\mathrm{cop}}
that is induced from the
\ell
-functional. Of course, this Lax operator is now simply equal to the
R
-matrix. However, we will see in Section
3
that this is no longer true for the long-range deformations. For this paper, we will solely be interested in evaluation-zero representations, i.e. we will set
v=0
, and write
\mathscr{L}(u)\coloneq\mathscr{L}(u,0)
. Since
\mathscr{L}
corresponds to a representation of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{\mathrm{cop}}
, it satisfies the
RLL-relation

\mathcal{R}_{ab}(u,v)\mathscr{L}_{a1}(u)\mathscr{L}_{b1}(v)=\mathscr{L}_{b1}(v)\mathscr{L}_{a1}(u)\mathcal{R}_{ab}(u,v),

(33)

which ensures the integrability of the associated spin chain
39
(see also Section
2.4
).

2.4
The Bethe subalgebra and charge densities

The RTT relation (
19
) for the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
gives rise to a large abelian subalgebra, which corresponds to an (infinite) tower of commuting charges in the associated physical spin chain for a given representation. In particular, the trace over
\mathrm{End}(V)
in the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}(u)
(
16
) defines the elements
t^{(-k)}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
given by

t(u)\coloneq\text{tr}_{\mathrm{End}(V)}\mathcal{T}(u)=1+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t^{(-k-1)}u^{k}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})[\![u]\!].

(34)

By taking the trace over the RTT-equation (
19
), it then immediately follows that all these elements commute, i.e.
[t(u),t(v)]=0
, or
[t^{(-k)},t^{(-\ell)}]=0
for
k,\ell\geq 1

89
;
90
;
95
. The elements
t^{(-k)}
therefore generate a (large) abelian subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, called the
Bethe subalgebra

81
. To make contact with the
local charges
that are generated by the Bethe subalgebra on specific spin chains, one often defines the elements

q_{k}\coloneq\frac{d^{k-2}}{du^{k-2}}\left(t(u)^{-1}\frac{d}{du}t(u)\right)\bigg|_{u=0}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\quad\text{for }k\geq 2,

(35)

such that the Bethe subalgebra is now generated by
t^{(-1)}
and
q_{k}
for
k\geq 2
, and the elements
q_{k}
are called the
charges
.
9
9

9

Often, one also considers the first charge
q_{1}=\log(1+t^{(-1)})
, where the logarithm should be interpreted as the Taylor expansion of
\log(1+x)
around
x=0
. Note that these charges are only well-defined in the completion
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
of the FRT-bialgebra as given under Definition
2.1
.
In the context of spin chains, one of these charges in the corresponding representation is then defined to be the
Hamiltonian

H
of a system. In most cases, this Hamiltonian is specifically identified with the second charge
q_{2}
. Since the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
corresponds to the dual of a Drinfeld-Jimbo algebra, this also implies that the Hamiltonian is given by an element of the dual of the quantised universal enveloping algebra. For example, for the Yangian, one has
H\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}

33
. Importantly, the existence of a large tower of pair-wise commuting charges helps with finding the spectral decomposition of the Hamiltonian, which lies at the root of the quantum inverse scattering method
89
;
95
;
43
;
90
;
39
;
91
;
64
;
92
;
62
;
40
.

Let us now consider these charges in the representation given by an
L
-fold tensor product of evaluation
0
representations, i.e.
\rho_{0}^{\otimes L}
, on
V^{\otimes L}
. Firstly, note that the evaluation of
\mathcal{T}(u)
under this representation, which we denote by
\mathcal{T}^{\rho}(u)
, is given by

\mathcal{T}_{a}^{\rho}(u)\coloneq\rho_{0}^{\otimes L}(\mathcal{T}_{a}(u))=\mathbf{r}(\mathcal{T}_{a}(u),\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{L}(0))=\mathscr{L}_{a,L}(u)\mathscr{L}_{a,L-1}(u)\cdots\mathscr{L}_{a,1}(u),

(36)

where
1,...,L
denote the coordinates on the
physical space

V^{\otimes L}
, while
a
denotes the coordinate on the auxiliary space
V
of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}(u)
. The matrix
\mathcal{T}_{a}^{\rho}(u)\in\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes L})
is the
monodromy matrix
. Similarly, one can consider the representation of the Bethe subalgebra, which is obtained by tracing the monodromy matrix over the auxiliary space. That is, one has

t^{\rho}(u)\coloneq\rho_{0}^{\otimes L}(t(u))=\text{tr}_{a}\mathcal{T}^{\rho}_{a}(u),

(37)

which is the
transfer matrix
. Since also in this representation one has
[t^{\rho}(u),t^{\rho}(v)]=0
, the charges
q_{k}
under this representation correspond to a set of commuting charges on
V^{\otimes L}
, and therefore give rise to a quantum integrable system. Moreover, since the
R
-matrix is regular, these charges are
local
. In particular, since
\mathscr{L}_{ak}(0)=\mathcal{P}_{ak}
, it can be noted that
t^{\rho}(0)=\mathcal{P}_{1,L}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{1,2}
is equal to the
shift operator

\mathcal{U}\colon e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{L}\mapsto e_{L}\otimes e_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{L-1}
on
V^{\otimes L}
, such that, for example, the second charge is given by (see e.g.
26
)

\mathbb{Q}_{2}\coloneq\rho_{0}^{\otimes L}(q_{2})=\mathcal{U}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{L}\text{tr}_{a}\left(\mathscr{L}_{a,L}(0)\cdots\dot{\mathscr{L}}_{a,k}(0)\cdots\mathscr{L}_{a,1}(0)\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{k,k+1},

(38)

where
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}\coloneq\mathscr{L}^{-1}(0)\dot{\mathscr{L}}(0)\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes 2})
denotes the
charge density
, with
\dot{\mathscr{L}}(u)\coloneq\frac{d}{du}\mathscr{L}(u)
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{i,j}
denotes the action of the charge density on the
i
-th and
j
-th components of the tensor product
V^{\otimes L}
. The second charge
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
in this representation is therefore simply a sum of the local densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}
of range
2
over the full spin chain
V^{\otimes L}
.

In fact, it is more generally true that the charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}\coloneq\rho_{0}^{\otimes L}(q_{k})
can be written as a sum over local densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
of range
k

26
,
10
10

10

Note here that the
k
-th charge will be denoted with a subscript of
k
, i.e.
q_{k}
, while the
k
-th charge density is denoted with a superscript of
k
in
mathfrak
, i.e.
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
.
where it should be noted that such local charge densities are not unique. Indeed, one may add terms to the charge density that vanish when summed over the whole spin chain. We then say that two charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)},\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
are equivalent, denoted by
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}\sim\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}
, if they generate the same (global) charges on any spin chain of length
L
, i.e. if
\sum_{n=1}^{L}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1}=\sum_{n=1}^{L}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1}
for all
L\geq k
. In particular, we have the following definition:

2.4 Definition
.

Let
k\geq 2
. Two charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)},\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
are said to be equivalent, denoted by
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}\sim\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}
, if there exists a matrix
\mathfrak{m}\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes(k-1)})
such that
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}=\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}+I_{V}\otimes\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{m}\otimes I_{V}
.

Note here that, by definition, we can only have equivalence between charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(\ell)}
if
k=\ell
.

We remark here that the origin of the locality of the charges is two-fold. Firstly, it is a consequence of the fact that the
R
-matrix is regular. Secondly, the charges of
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
are of range
k
precisely because of the factorisation property (
27
) of the
r
-form, which allows the monodromy matrix (
36
) to be given in terms of the “range-two” Lax operators. In particular, all the higher charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
are also sums and commutants of range-two terms. For example, the third charge density is given by (see e.g.
26
)

\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{123}=[\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{23},\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12}]+\frac{d}{du}(\mathscr{L}^{-1}_{12}(u)\dot{\mathscr{L}}_{12}(u))\big|_{u=0}.

(39)

The fact that the origin of the local charges comes from regularity and the factorisation property of the
r
-form now already gives us a hint towards what the correct description for the long-range deformations should be. In particular, one expects that long-range deformations will appear if the Lax operator cannot fully factorise into a product of range-two terms, which, in light of the discussion in Section
2.3
, happens if the associativity of the FRT-bialgebra is explicitly broken. Indeed, as we will see in the Section
3
, the breaking of associativity is exactly what gives rise to the long-range deformations.

2.5
Algebraic charge densities

Before we can describe the long-range deformations of the FRT-bialgebras, we will introduce some algebra elements that we will call the
algebraic charge densities
, and which are given in terms of derivations of the
R
-matrix. In fact, these objects can already be used to give us more information about the FRT-bialgebra and the corresponding integrable model as well. In particular, as we will see, these algebraic charge densities can be thought of as algebraic generalisations of the ordinary charge densities. In particular, we will give a conjecture for the explicit expressions of the charge densities using the algebraic charge densities. Moreover, these objects will be used to derive the higher-order Sutherland equations in Section
2.6
and in the description of the bialgebra deformations in Section
3
.

Firstly, let us consider the differential operator
\mathcal{D}\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}\to\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
given by

(\mathcal{D}f)\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}(u_{1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}(u_{N})\right)\coloneq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{d}{dv_{n}}f\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}(v_{N})\right)\bigg|_{(v_{1},...,v_{N})=(u_{1},...,u_{N})}

(40)

for
f\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
. In the context of the dual pairing (
24
) as described in Section
2.2
, this derivation corresponds to an operator on the quantised universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. In particular, one may define the corresponding operator
\mathcal{D}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
given by
\mathcal{D}(x)=\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{B}_{u}(x)\big|_{u=0}
for
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{u}
is the boost automorphism. It can be easily verified that
\mathcal{D}
is indeed a
derivation
, i.e.
\mathcal{D}(xy)=\mathcal{D}(x)y+x\mathcal{D}(y)
. Moreover, it is a
coderivation
, as under the coproduct of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, one has
\Delta(\mathcal{D}(x))=(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{D})\Delta(x)
, which, after repeated coproducts, gives the same formula as (
40
) in the dual picture. We will write
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for the derivative on the
i
-th coordinate. Note here that, since the universal
R
-matrix for the Yangian is of difference-form, it satisfies
(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})(\mathcal{R})=0
. For general
R
-matrices that are not of difference form, this obviously does not have to hold.
11
11

11

Remark that
\mathcal{D}
is only a well-defined map
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
to
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
as bialgebras if the
R
-matrix is of difference form such that it commutes with the derivation. Otherwise,
\mathcal{D}
should be regarded as a derivation on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
as an algebra. Note that it is well-defined as a derivation over a bialgebra when restricted to the subspace of functions over
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
generated by elements
t_{ij}(0)
(see Section
3.3
).

We can now use the derivation on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})^{*}
to define the following bilinear maps:

2.5 Definition
.

For
k\geq 2
, the
algebraic charge densities
are the bilinear maps
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)},\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{C}
given by

\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}\coloneq\mathcal{D}^{k-2}_{1}\left(\mathbf{r}^{-1}\ast\mathcal{D}_{1}(\mathbf{r})\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\coloneq-\mathcal{D}^{k-2}_{2}\left(\mathbf{r}^{-1}\ast\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathbf{r})\right),

where
\mathcal{D}_{1}\coloneq\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathrm{id}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}\coloneq\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{D}
.

Note that
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
is defined with an additional minus sign, which will be argued for later. In the dual picture as described in Section
2.2
, the above bilinear maps correspond to the elements
(-1)^{m-1}\mathcal{D}^{k-2}_{i}\left(\mathcal{R}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{m}(\mathcal{R})\right)
for
m=1,2
, where
\mathcal{R}
is the universal
R
-matrix as given above (
22
). Since
\mathbf{r}(1,a)=\varepsilon(a)=\mathbf{r}(a,1)
, it can also be remarked that
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,1)=0=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(1,a)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(a,1)=0=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(1,a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. By evaluating the algebraic charge densities at products of the matrix corepresentations
\mathcal{T}_{i}(u_{i})
, they define new matrices given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M})&\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}\right)\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes M}),\\
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,N)(N+1,...,M)}(u_{1},...,u_{M})&\coloneq\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\left(\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}\right)\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes M})\end{split}

(41)

for
0<N<M
, where we used the shorthand notation
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(u_{i})
on the right-hand side. In the dual picture, these matrix functions correspond to specific evaluation representations of coproducts of the elements
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}\coloneq\mathcal{D}_{1}^{k-2}(\mathcal{R}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{1}(\mathcal{R}))
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\coloneq-\mathcal{D}^{k-2}_{2}(\mathcal{R}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{R}))
. From now on, we will be less explicit about the correspondence between these matrix functions and elements in a representation of the corresponding dual algebra. Moreover, we will often suppress the explicit dependence on the spectral parameters to simplify notation.

In order to get some intuition behind the algebraic charge densities, let us first restrict to
k=2
, i.e. the elements
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}
. Using braiding unitarity
\mathcal{R}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{21}=1
, it can be noted that

\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{12}^{(2)}=-\mathcal{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{12})=-\mathcal{R}_{21}\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{21}^{-1})=\mathcal{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{21}\mathcal{R}_{12},

(42)

which shows that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}
are in fact related by conjugation. Moreover, since
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{D}_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{12})
, it immediately follows that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(\mathbf{0})=\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12}=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{12}(\mathbf{0})
, where
\mathbf{0}=(0,0)
and the charge density
\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12}
was defined under (
38
). This gives the reason why we refer to
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}
as algebraic charge densities; they restrict to the actual charge densities that appear in the tower of commuting charges, but are now defined for arbitrary spectral parameters
u_{i}
and arbitrary coproducts. Note that, in the case the
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}
is of difference form, such that
(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})(\mathcal{R})=0
, we have
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}=\mathcal{Q}
, thus motivating the additional minus sign in the definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Let us now consider the coproducts of the algebraic charge densities
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}
. Firstly, the factorisation property of the
r
-form (
27
), or dually, the factorisation property of the universal
R
-matrix (
22
), allows us to write the coproducts of
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}
in terms of the “two-legged” algebraic charge density. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(23)}\mathcal{D}_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{1(23)})=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{13}\mathcal{D}_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}+\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12},

(43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{(12)3}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(12)3}(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})(\mathcal{R}_{(12)3})=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{13}(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})(\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23})\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}+\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{23},

and similarly

\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{1(23)}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{12}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{(12)3}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{23},

(44)

where we recall the notation for the brackets in the subscript for the coproduct as introduced under (
29
). Note here that the regularity of the
R
-matrix implies that the coproducts of the algebraic charge density are given by
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(\mathbf{0})=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{(12)3}(\mathbf{0})=\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12}+\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{23}
, such that the coproducts evaluated at trivial spectral parameters gives a sum over the charge densities.

We can now generalise the formula for the coproduct of the algebraic charge densities to arbitrary values of
k\geq 2
. In particular, as shown in Appendix
A.1
, one has the following result:

2.6 Proposition
.

For
k\geq 2
, the coproduct relations for the algebraic charge densities
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
are given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(23)}&=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=k-1}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right],\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(12)3}&=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{23}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=k-1}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{23},\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-2}})}_{23},\left[\cdots,\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{23}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})},\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{13}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}\mathcal{R}_{23}\right]\cdots\right]\right]\right],\end{split}

where
\overline{\ell_{i}}=\ell_{i}+1
and
\ell_{i}\geq 1
and
c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\coloneq\prod_{m=2}^{n}\dbinom{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{i}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\ell_{i}}
.

We remark that (very) similar coproduct relations may be derived in an analogous way as in Appendix
A.1
for
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{(12)3}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{1(23)}
.
12
12

12

Remark that these coproducts are then only well-defined as “true” coproducts. i.e. are intertwined by the
R
-matrix, if the
R
-matrix is of difference form (see footnote
11
). The coproducts as given in Proposition
2.6
are well-defined even if the
R
-matrix is not of difference form, since here the derivation and the coproduct in
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
act on the two different legs.
However, we will not need the explicit expressions for this paper.

Let us now consider the explicit case for
k=3
, in which case it follows from Proposition
2.6
that the coproducts are given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}&=\mathcal{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}+\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{12}+[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}],\\
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(3)}_{(12)3}&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(3)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(3)}_{23}+[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{23},\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}].\end{split}

(45)

If we now set all the spectral parameters to
0
, in which case the
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}
reduces to the permutation operator, it can be directly seen from (
39
) that

\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}(\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{23}(\mathbf{0})\right)\sim\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{123}\sim\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(3)}_{(12)3}(\mathbf{0})-\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(3)}_{12}(\mathbf{0})\right),

(46)

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation for charge densities as given in Definition
2.4
. As already suggested in the naming, the algebraic charge densities for
k=3
are therefore directly related to the charge densities for the commuting charges as described in Section
2.4
. Remark here that (
46
) gives two different representatives for the third charge density.

It is now expected that a similar formula as (
46
) holds true for all higher-order charge densities as well. In particular, we have the following conjecture:

2.7 Conjecture
.

For
k\geq 2
, the charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}
are given by

\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})\right)\sim\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}\sim\left(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-1)k}(\mathbf{0})-\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-2)k-1}(\mathbf{0})\right),

with
\sim
the equivalence relation as given in Definition
2.4
.

As of this moment, we do not have a proof for this conjecture. Proving the result would require one to explicitly calculate each charge density from the transfer matrix (
37
) or using the boost formalism
96
;
69
(see also e.g.
71
;
27
;
26
) and then compare the result to the equation of the coproducts of charge densities using Proposition
2.6
. Calculating each of these charge densities explicitly seems to be a rather tedious endeavour, which is why we have not attempted to prove the conjecture at this point. We do, however, have strong evidence for the conjecture to be true. For example, we have explicitly verified the equality in
Mathematica
for specific
R
-matrices/models. More importantly, it is shown in Corollary
2.12
that the charges that are defined through the above conjecture all commute with the transfer matrix. Moreover, the long-range deformations that we will describe in Section
3
are expected to exactly correspond to the family of long-range deformations generated by the boost and bilocal operators (see Section
3.1
or
4
;
5
). This equivalence between the two deformation equations on the level of charge densities is only true if Conjecture
2.7
holds. We remark that none of the results in the following sections depend on this conjecture. Of course, assuming the conjecture to be true, Proposition
2.6
then allows one to give a recurrence relation for all the charge densities in terms of lower charge densities:

2.8 Corollary
.

The charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
for
k\geq 2
satisfy the recurrence relation

\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}+\sum_{n=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=k-1}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\mathfrak{q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{2,3,...,\overline{\ell_{n}}+1},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right],

where
\overline{\ell_{i}}=\ell_{i}+1
,
\ell_{i}\geq 1
,
\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell})}_{12}\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell})}_{12}(\mathbf{0})
and
c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}
as given in Proposition
2.6
.

The appearance of the difference
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})
in the expression for the charge density in Conjecture
2.7
is not accidental. In fact, it is related to an important property of the algebraic charge densities that will be used many times in the various proofs in this paper. In order to describe this property, we will first need to introduce some notation. Namely, in the following, we will often encounter situations where we have a mixture of trivial and non-trivial insertions of spectral parameters. In order to explicitly make this distinction and simplify notation, we will denote a coordinate with a non-trivial spectral parameter by an underline. That is,
