\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}(u_{1})
, while
\mathcal{T}_{1}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)
. Moreover, for a vector
\mathbf{a}\coloneq(a_{1},...,a_{|\mathbf{a}|})
we write
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(u_{\mathbf{a}})\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}_{1}}(u_{1})\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}_{2}}(u_{2})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}_{|\mathbf{a}|}}(u_{|\mathbf{a}|})
. We then consider the specific elements

\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,N,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}),\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(\underline{\mathbf{a}},1,...,N-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})N}\coloneq\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N-1}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}},\mathcal{T}_{N}),

(47)

for
N\geq 2
and where we used the simplified notation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(u_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}})
,
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}(u_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
. A similar notation is used for
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
. As shown in Appendix
A
, we have the following result:

2.9 Lemma
.

For
k\geq 2
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}&=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k-1)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k-1)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\\
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(\underline{\mathbf{a}},1,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})k}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}k}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(\underline{\mathbf{a}},1,...,k-1)k}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}k}&=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})k}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}k}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-1)k}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}k}\end{split}

where
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{|\mathbf{a}|})
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},...,b_{|\mathbf{b}|})
. Moreover, the underline in the above equation denotes a non-trivial insertion of a spectral parameter, while the other coordinates have a trivial spectral parameter
0
.

In particular, this shows that such differences of algebraic charge densities have, in some sense, a “finite length”
k
. In order to simplify notation even more, we introduce:

2.10 Definition
.

For
k\geq 2
, the
reduced algebraic charge densities
are the trilinear maps
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}(k),{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\colon\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{C}
given by

\begin{split}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,b,c)&\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,b\cdot c)-(\mathbf{r}^{-1}_{12}\ast\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\ast\mathbf{r}_{12})(a,b,c)\\
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(a,b,c)&\coloneq\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(a\cdot b,c)-(\mathbf{r}^{-1}_{23}\ast\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\ast\mathbf{r}_{23})(a,b,c).\end{split}

Note here that
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(1,b,c)=0={}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,1,c)
and similarly
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(a,b,1)=0={}^{r}\tilde{Q}^{(k)}(a,1,c)
for all
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Moreover, we have
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,b,1)=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}(a,b)
and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(1,b,c)=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}(b,c)
. Importantly, Lemma
2.9
now immediately implies that

\begin{split}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}={}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(2,...,k-1)}\quad\text{and}\quad{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(\underline{\mathbf{a}},1,...,k-1),\underline{\mathbf{b}},k}={}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-1),\underline{\mathbf{b}},k},\end{split}

(48)

such that the reduced algebraic charge densities now have, in some sense, a “finite length”. These reduced algebraic charge densities will play an important role in the derivation of the generalised Sutherland equations in Section
2.6
and in the proofs for the twists of the quantum algebras in Section
3
.

2.6
Generalised Sutherland equations

The higher-order algebraic charge densities now allow us to derive higher-order variants of the Sutherland equation
94
, which will play an important role in the integrable long-range deformations. In particular, as we will see in Section
3.2
, they can be used to derive the Lax operators for the long-range deformations up to first order in the deformation parameter. Moreover, as we will see in Section
3.3
, the equations appear in the proofs for the associativity of the corresponding deformed algebras.

Let us first recall the Sutherland equation for the second charge. By taking the derivation of the Yang-Baxter equation, one can derive

\begin{split}&\mathcal{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}-\mathcal{R}_{23}\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}\right)=0\\
\Rightarrow\quad&[\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{23},\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}]=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{21}\mathcal{R}_{12}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{21}\mathcal{R}_{23}\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\end{split}

(49)

By considering the trivial spectral parameters
0
in coordinates
2
and
3
, such that
\mathcal{R}_{23}
becomes the permutation operator, this equation reduces to the known
94
(see also e.g.
71
;
27
;
26
) Sutherland equation

[\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{12},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}(12)}]=\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a}2}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}1}-\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}2}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a}1},

(50)

where
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a}1}\coloneq\frac{d}{dv}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}1}(u,v)\big|_{v=0}
.

We can now use the algebraic charge densities as described in the previous section to derive the higher-order Sutherland equations. Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{|\underline{\mathbf{a}}|})
such that the
R
-matrix in the coordinates
\mathbf{a}
is given by
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}1}=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}_{1}1}\cdots\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}_{|\mathbf{a}|}1}
, where we use the underline to emphasise a non-trivial insertion of a spectral parameter. For
k\geq 2
, we then have

\begin{split}[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}]&=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(12)}-\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{a}})}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}+\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}2}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}1}\\
&=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}2}\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2)}-\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}1}-\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{a}})}-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)},\end{split}

(51)

where in the first equality we used that
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}2}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2)}=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2{\underline{\mathbf{a}}})}\mathcal{R}_{{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}2}
. Now let
k\geq 3
. By taking a
(k-2)
-th coproduct on the second coordinate, one readily obtains

\begin{split}[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)}]&=\mathcal{R}_{{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(2,..,k)}\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}1}\\
&\quad-\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k,\underline{\mathbf{a}})}-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)},\end{split}

(52)

and similarly

\begin{split}[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)}]&=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(3,...,k)}\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(\underline{\mathbf{a}},3,...,k)}-\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}\\
&\quad-\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k,\underline{\mathbf{a}})}-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}\right]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)}.\end{split}

(53)

Analogous expressions can be obtained for a commutation relation between the
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}
and algebraic charge density
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
. Let us now use Conjecture
2.7
as a definition for the charge densities. That is, we define two representatives for the
k
-th charge density by

\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}\coloneq\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-1)k}(\mathbf{0})-\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-2)k-1}(\mathbf{0}).

(54)

By taking the difference between (
52
) and (
53
), considering trivial spectral parameters on the coordinates
1,...,k
, and by applying Lemma
2.9
, we have the following immediate result (for
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
mutatis mutandis):

2.11 Proposition
.

Let
k\geq 3
, then the generalised Sutherland equations are given by

\begin{split}[\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)}]&=\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(2,...,k)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(2,...,k-1)}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}1}-\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(3,...,k)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(3,...,k)}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)},\\
[\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{1,...,k},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k)}]&=\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}},(k-1,k)}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,k-2),\underline{\mathbf{a}},k-1}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,k-2)}-\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}k}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(2,...,k-1),\underline{\mathbf{a}},k}\mathscr{L}_{{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(1,...,k-1)},\end{split}

where
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
are the reduced algebraic charge densities as given in Definition
2.10
.

Remark here that the above proposition generalises the
k=2
Sutherland equation as given in (
49
). Moreover, it is immediately clear that the higher-order Sutherland equations depend on the chosen representative of the charge density
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}
. For the purposes of this paper, we will only need the above two representatives for the charge densities.

Importantly, these Sutherland equations can now be used to show that the charges defined through Conjecture
2.7
all commute with the transfer matrix. Indeed, using the fact that one obtains a (vanishing) telescoping sum when commuting the charges with the transfer matrix (see also Appendix
A.1
), we have the following immediate Corollary:

2.12 Corollary
.

For
k\geq 3
, let
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{2(3,...,k)}(\mathbf{0})
and consider the corresponding charge operator
\mathbb{Q}_{k}\coloneq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1}
, then
13
13

13

Remark here that
\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},m}(u)
denotes the monodromy matrix (
36
) on the doubly-infinite spin chain. Of course, one might wonder whether such an infinite product is well-defined. However, for the purposes of illustrating the result, we will assume that one could, in principle, make sense of this infinite product in some way, for which the result is not expected to depend on its specifics.

\left[\mathbb{Q}_{k},\mathrm{tr}_{a}\left(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},m}(u)\right)\right]=0.

In particular, this shows that the charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
that are
defined
through the charge densities as given by Conjecture
2.7
all commute with the transfer matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain. One could, in principle, generalise the above result to show that the charges commute with the transfer matrix on a finite spin chain. The fact that all these charges commute with the transfer matrix is, of course, great evidence that Conjecture
2.7
holds true.

3
Long-range deformed FRT-bialgebras

We now have all the necessary machinery to discuss the deformations of the FRT-bialgebras that correspond to the long-range integrable deformations of integrable spin chains. Let us first recall the notion of the long-range integrable models. For some deformation parameter
\lambda
, the deformed local charges are given by
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{Q}_{k}^{(n)}\lambda^{n}
such that
[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda),\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)]=0
, and where
\mathbb{Q}_{k}^{(0)}
is the undeformed charge as discussed in Section
2.4
. Moreover, for the deformation to be long-range, we impose that the higher correction terms have charge densities of range greater than
k
. In particular, the charge density for charge
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
can be formally expanded as
14
14

14

The corresponding operator
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
is then often also required to be
quasi-local
, see e.g.
53
;
54
.

\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{1,...,k}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{(k),(n)}_{1,...,k_{n}}\lambda^{n},

(55)

where
k_{n+1}>k_{n}
,
k_{0}=k
and
\mathfrak{q}^{(k),(n)}_{1,...,k_{n}}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k_{n}})
. That is, the range of the charge density increases (generally linearly) with the order in the deformation parameter
\lambda
. A family of such integrable long-range deformations of spin chains have been described in
8
;
4
;
5
. Although these deformations are well-understood on the level of the charges, the corresponding quantum groups had not yet been constructed. Some progress was made in
45
;
46
and
68
, where the Lax operator for some long-range deformation was discussed. We will now improve upon these results by explicitly constructing the full FRT-type bialgebras that correspond to these long-range deformations at first order in
\lambda
. In particular, in Section
3.1
, we will briefly recall the families of long-range deformations that were found in
4
;
5
. Then, in Section
3.2
, we use the higher-order Sutherland equations to derive the Lax operators for the long-range deformations up to first order in
\lambda
. In Section
3.3
, we discuss the corresponding deformations of the FRT-bialgebra that give rise to all the long-range deformations for the family of long-range deformations up to first order in
\lambda
. In particular, we give the explicit twisting elements for these deformations and show that the corresponding Drinfeld associators encode the information for the long-range interactions. In Section
3.4
, we then apply this to the XXX Heisenberg spin chain to provide the explicit expressions for the Lax operators and
R
-matrices of the boost
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
and the bilocal
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformation. Lastly, in Section
3.5
, we also discuss the long-range deformation for the Yangian algebra.

3.1
The deformation equation

Let us begin by reviewing the families of integrable long-range deformations that were described in
8
;
4
;
5
. Here, we will consider the charges on the doubly-infinite spin chain, such that
\mathbb{Q}_{k}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1}
. For a deformation parameter
\lambda
, we then consider the deformed charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{Q}_{k}^{(n)}\lambda^{n}
, where
\mathbb{Q}^{(0)}_{k}=\mathbb{Q}_{k}
, and the charges satisfy the deformation equation

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)=[\mathcal{X}(\lambda),\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)],

(56)

for some yet to be determined operator
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
on the doubly-infinite spin chain. Since
[\mathbb{Q}_{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}]=0
, it can be easily found that any new tower of charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
that satisfy (
56
) are also pairwise commuting, i.e.
[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda),\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)]=0
. However, we are not interested in just any deformation of the charges, but we require the deformed charges to act
locally
and
homogeneously
on the spin chain as well. That is, the charge
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
can be written as a sum over the local charge densities of the form given in (
55
). Three families of choices for
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
have been identified in
4
;
5
:

•

Local operators:
Firstly, let
k\geq 1
and consider a matrix
\mathfrak{m}_{1,...,k}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
, which defines a local operator
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}
on the doubly-infinite spin chain. If we then set
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, this will give rise to local and homogeneous long-range deformations of the integrable charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
. In fact, since
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
does now not depend on
\lambda
, the corresponding deformation equation (
56
) can now be integrated exactly to give
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)=\exp(\lambda\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}})\mathbb{Q}_{k}(0)\exp(-\lambda\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}})
. In particular, the deformed charge
\mathfrak{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}(\lambda)
is related to the undeformed charge via conjugation. Such a deformation on the level of the quantum group can be simply achieved by a change in representation. However, in Section
3.3
, we will still consider a deformation of the FRT-bialgebra that leads to the local-operator long-range deformation, as it is an easy example to consider to gain some additional intuition behind the other long-range quantum group deformations.

•

Boost operators:
For a long-range deformed charge
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
, we write its corresponding charge density as
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n}(\lambda)\coloneq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{q}^{(k),(m)}_{n,...,n+k_{m}-1}\lambda^{m}
, where
k_{m+1}>k_{m}
and
k_{0}=k
in line with (
55
), such that
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n}(\lambda)
. The
boost operator
corresponding to
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
is then given by

\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)]\coloneq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n}(\lambda).

(57)

Setting
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)=\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)]
for any
k\geq 3
will then give rise to a non-trivial long-range integrable local and homogeneous deformation of the charges. We remark here that
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
depends on the charge
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
, which itself depends on
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
through the deformation equation (
56
). In particular, one should now think of
(
56
)
as defining a recurrence relation for
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
, which can be solved iteratively at each order of
\lambda
. One might remark here that the charge densities
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n}(\lambda)
are not actually unique, and only defined up to the equivalence as given in Definition
2.4
. However, it can be easily verified that, for two different representatives
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}
for the charge density, the corresponding boost operators (
57
) differ by a local operator.

•

Bilocal operators:
Lastly, for
2\leq k<\ell
, we consider the
bilocal operator
15
15

15

We remark here that our definition for the bilocal operator is different from the one used in
4
;
5
. However, it is easy to verify that the definitions differ from each other by a local operator. We use our definition, as it is better suited for the deformation of the FRT-algebra that we will describe in Section
3.3
.

[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)]\coloneq\sum_{n\leq m}\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n+\ell-k}(\lambda)\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m}(\lambda).

(58)

Setting
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)=[\mathbb{Q}_{l}(\lambda)|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)]
then gives rise to non-trivial long-range integrable local and homogeneous charges. Again,
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
depends on the charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda),\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)
, which themselves in turn depend on
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
through the deformation equation (
56
). Therefore, also for the bilocal operator, the deformation equation (
56
) defines a recurrence relation for the charges. Moreover, it can be noted that by choosing a different representative for the local charge densities, the corresponding bilocal operators will differ by a local operator. Lastly, by substituting
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n}\to 1
in (
58
), it can be seen that one obtains the boost operator (
57
). Therefore, one can view the long-range deformation corresponding to the boost operator as a special case of the deformation corresponding to the bilocal operator. We will see in Section
3.3
that this remains true for the deformation of the quantum group.

Additionally,
5
introduces a change of basis of the charges, i.e.
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)\to\Lambda_{k}^{\ell}(\lambda)\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)
, as a long-range deformation. However, such a change of basis transformation does not realise a truly inequivalent tower of commuting charges, and we will therefore not consider it as a true deformation. In fact, such a deformation is also not realisable on the level of the FRT-bialgebra, as its generated charges should always increase in range. The closest that one can get to a change of basis is a reparametrisation of the spectral parameter, i.e.
u\to f_{\lambda}(u)
for some
\lambda
dependent function. The spin chain might also have some additional Lie algebra symmetry, which in principle provides additional homogeneous local charges. The deformation corresponding to the boost operator for these Lie algebra charges will, in general, give rise to a deformation of nearest-neighbour type, i.e. non-long-range. However, the bilocal operators involving such charges do give rise to additional long-range deformations
13
.

The above families of long-range deformations can now be combined to obtain a moduli space of long-range deformations. In particular, one can define

\mathcal{X}(\lambda)\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{n}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{n}+\alpha^{k}_{n}\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)]+\beta^{k|\ell}_{n}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\lambda)]\right]

(59)

for a general long-range deformation, where
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
is a (possibly zero) local operator, and the indices
k
and
\ell
are summed over. Again, one should remember here that
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
is dependent on the charges
\mathbb{Q}_{k}(\lambda)
, which themselves are dependent on
\mathcal{X}(\lambda)
. For the purposes of this paper, we will only be interested in the first-order correction to the charges. In particular, we will only consider
\mathcal{X}(0)
in the deformation equation (
56
), which is generated by some local operator
\mathfrak{m}
, the boost operators
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(0)]
and the bilocal operators
[\mathbb{Q}_{k}(0)|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(0)]
.

3.2
Lax operators for the long-range deformations

To obtain some intuition behind the FRT-bialgebras that will describe the long-range deformations, we will first derive the corresponding Lax operators at first order in the deformation parameter
\lambda
. Let us first introduce some notation. We consider the monodromy matrix on the infinite spin chain, which we denote by the infinite (co)product
\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}(u)\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},m}(u)
.
16
16

16

See footnote
13
.
Similarly, we define the Lax operators on the half-infinite chains by
\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n,...)}(u)\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},m}(u)
and
\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...,n)}(u)\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq n}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},m}(u)
. If one now considers the commutant between the boost operator
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{2}]
for the second charge and the monodromy matrix, one obtains

\begin{split}[\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{2}],\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot[\mathfrak{q}^{(2)}_{n,n+1},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]\\
&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n+2,...)}\left[\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a},n+1}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},n}-\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},n+1}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a},n}\right]\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...,n-1)}=-\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a},(...)},\end{split}

(60)

where
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\underline{a}n}\coloneq\frac{d}{dv}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}n}(u,v)\big|_{v=0}
, and in the last step we did the substitution
n\to n-1
in the first term, which then cancels with the second term to obtain a sum over derivatives in the
n
-th coordinate, and hence a total derivative of the monodromy matrix. Of course, this is a well-known result (see e.g
71
), giving rise to the boost formalism for generating the higher-order charges from just the second charge
96
;
69
.

Using the higher-order Sutherland equations as given in Proposition
2.11
, one can now easily generalise this result to compute the commutants between general boost or bilocal operators and the monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain. In particular, for
k\geq 3
, one readily obtains

\begin{split}[\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}],\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\cdot[\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}(n+1,...)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{n,\underline{a},(n+1,...,n+k-2)}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...,n)}.\end{split}

(61)

Similarly, for
2\leq k<\ell
, we have

\begin{split}&[[\mathbb{Q}_{k}|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}],\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]=\sum_{n\leq m}[\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n+\ell-k,...,n+\ell-1}\cdot\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m,...,m+\ell-1},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]\\
=&\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n+\ell-k,...,n+\ell-1}\cdot\big[\sum_{m\geq n}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m,...,m+\ell-1},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}\big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ +\big[\sum_{m\leq n}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{m+\ell-k,...,m+\ell-1},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}\big]\cdot\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{n,...,n+\ell-1}\big)\\
=&\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n+\ell,...)}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n+\ell-k,...,n+\ell-1}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n+1,...,n+\ell-1)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{n,\underline{a},(n+1,...,n+\ell-2)}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...,n)}\\
&\qquad-\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n+\ell-1,...)}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(n+\ell-k+1,...,n+\ell-2),\underline{a},n+\ell-1}\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(n,...,n+\ell-2)}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{n,...,n+\ell-1}\mathscr{L}_{a,(...,n-1)}\big),\end{split}

(62)

where we recall that
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
are the reduced algebraic charge densities as given in Definition
2.10
. Remark that we use the two different representatives
\tilde{q}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}
for the charge densities. This choice of representatives will lead to a more natural form of the FRT-bialgebra deformation as described in Section
3.3
.

Using the above formula, one can readily write down the corresponding Lax operators that generate the deformed monodromy matrices. Before writing down the corresponding Lax operators, let us first introduce some notation. In particular, as noted in
45
;
46
;
68
, and as we will see in this section, to describe long-range charges in a Lax-operator formalism, one needs to increase the auxiliary space of the Lax operator. Therefore, for
m\geq 1
we define

\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{m})n}(u)\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{m})n}(u,0,...,0)=\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a}n}(u)\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},n}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{b_{m},n},

(63)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the permutation operator, and where we used the factorisation of the
R
-matrix as given in (
29
). Recall here the notation as introduced in Section
2.5
that an underline in the coordinate corresponds to a non-trivial insertion of a spectral parameter, while no underline signifies a trivial spectral parameter
0
. In light of the above notation, we will also write
\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{m})n}\coloneq\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},n}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{b_{m},n}
and
\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{m})(...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{m})n}
, which is just a shift operator on the auxiliary space and the doubly-infinite spin chain. The Lax operators corresponding to the long-range deformations are now given as follows:

•

Local operator:
let
k\geq 2
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
, then the Lax operator corresponding to the local operator deformation up to first order in
\lambda
is given by

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})n}&\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})n}+\lambda\Big(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{k-1})}\mathfrak{m}_{n,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}R_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{k-1})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{n\underline{a}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}\mathcal{R}_{n\underline{a}}\Big)\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})n}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}).\end{split}

(64)

Indeed, if we define the corresponding monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})(...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})n}
, then it can be readily calculated by bringing all the permutation operators to right side, that the first-order correction is given by
\mathscr{L}^{(1)}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})(...)}=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]\times\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{k-1})(...)}
. In particular, this shows that the first-order correction to the monodromy matrix is given by the commutator of the monodromy matrix with a local operator, times a shift operator. When calculating the charges, this shift operator will be insignificant, such that indeed all the charges will correspond to the local operator long-range deformation as described in Section
3.1
.

•

Boost operator:
let
k\geq 3
, then the Lax operator corresponding to the boost operator deformation up to first order in
\lambda
is given by

\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}+\lambda\left({}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{n,\underline{a},(b_{1},...,b_{k-2})}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}).

(65)

Again, one can consider the monodromy matrix
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})(...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}
, for which the first-order correction, by bringing all the permutation operators to the right, can be calculated to be given by
\mathscr{L}^{(1)}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})(...)}=[\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}],\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]\times\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{k-2})(...)}
, where the commutator between the boost operator and the monodromy matrix was given in (
61
). Therefore, this Lax operator will indeed generate all the charges corresponding to the boost operator-generated long-range deformation.

•

Bilocal operator:
Let
2\leq k<\ell
, the Lax operator corresponding to the bilocal operator deformation up to first order in
\lambda
is given by

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})n}&\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})n}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\\
&+\lambda\Big(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{b_{\ell-k},...,b_{\ell-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{n,\underline{a},(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})n}\\
&\qquad-\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})n}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1}),\underline{a},n}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1},n}\Big).\end{split}

(66)

Again, when we consider the monodromy matrix
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})(...)}\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})n}
, it can be derived that
\mathscr{L}^{(1)}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})(...)}=[[\mathbb{Q}_{k}|\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}],\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]\times\mathcal{P}_{(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})(...)}
, where the commutator between the bilocal operator and the monodromy matrix was given in (
62
). Therefore, this Lax operator indeed generates the long-range deformed charges corresponding to the bilocal operator.

As discussed in the previous section, the undeformed model might possess some additional Lie algebra symmetry, which also provides local homogeneous charges. Bilocal operators of the form
[\mathbb{Q}_{k}|J]
, where
J
is a Lie algebra generator, then also give rise to long-range deformations
13
. The Lax operators for these long-range deformations can, in principle, be derived analogously, using a Sutherland-type equation for the charge
J
and the commutator
[\Delta(J)_{12},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}]
. We remark that these equations are model-dependent and will therefore not be discussed in this paper. However, we emphasise that all the results can easily be generalised to include these types of Lie algebra charges and corresponding long-range deformations for specific
R
-matrices.

It should be noted that the above Lax operators can now also be used to construct the charges on spin chains of finite length
L
, in which case all the corresponding charge densities will still have the same long-range corrections. Namely, the long-range charge densities on the finite-length chains can now appear due to the increased auxiliary space. By computing the monodromy matrix
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{m})L}\cdots\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{m})1}
and tracing over the increased auxiliary space for the transfer matrix, the
wrapping corrections
, when the interaction range exceeds the length of the spin chain, of the long-range charge densities will occur
45
;
46
;
68
. Lastly, we note that the deformed Lax operators are now, in general, not regular. For example, the Lax operator for the boost operator
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{k}]
deformation evaluated at
u=0
is given by

\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}(0)=\left(1+\lambda\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2}}\right)\mathcal{P}_{(a,b_{1},...,b_{k-2})n}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(67)

which is therefore not exactly equal to a shift operator. Of course, this is expected as the commutator between the boost operator and the shift operator is non-zero, such that the first charge
\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\log(\mathcal{U})
should also get a correction. Therefore, these Lax operators do not fall into the class of long-range Lax operators that were described in
45
;
46
. Although the Lax operator is not regular, it should be noted that locality of the charges is still ensured, as the Lax operator is regular at zeroth order in
\lambda
.

3.3
Deformation of the FRT-bialgebra

As described in Section
2
, the Lax operators for Yang-Baxter integrable spin chains appear as representations of an associated FRT-bialgebra. Therefore, we would like to find the FRT-type bialgebras that give rise to the long-range deformed Lax operators that we described in the previous section. In particular, one expects the Lax operator to arise as a representation of a deformation of the FRT-bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Here, the form of the Lax operators as given in (
64
), (
65
), and (
66
) already gives us some clues towards what such a deformation should look like. Firstly, the fact that the auxiliary space needs to be increased with coordinates with trivial spectral parameters also suggests that the algebra elements in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
need to be multiplied by some “trivial elements”. We will show that this increasing of the auxiliary space corresponds on the level of the FRT-bialgebra to taking a so called “double-crossed product” with another bialgebra. Moreover, one can see in (
66
) that the Lax operator looks very similar to one that is obtained from a (Drinfeld) twisted bialgebra
30
. Indeed, we will see that the long-range deformations correspond to a twist of the double-crossed product algebra. However, as we discussed at the end of Section
2.4
, and as we will see in this Section, this algebra will not be (fully) associative in order to describe the long-range deformation.

Let us begin by discussing the double-crossed product construction, which naturally corresponds to increasing the size of the auxiliary space. Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
be an FRT-bialgebra and let
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
be the free algebra generated by
t_{ij}^{(-1)}
, i.e. the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
consisting of sums of products of elements
t_{ij}(0)
that correspond to the spectral parameter set to
u=0
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, it also naturally inherits a bialgebra structure and a Hopf algebra structure under the completion to
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\circ}
. Moreover, the
r
-form is also well-defined over
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and naturally induces a right-action of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
on
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and a left action of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
given by

a\triangleleft b\coloneq\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})a_{(2)}\mathbf{r}(a_{(3)},b_{(2)})\quad\text{and}\quad a\triangleright b\coloneq\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})b_{(2)}\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},b_{(3)}),

(68)

for
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. We now have the following definition
75
;
74
(see also e.g.
73
;
58
;
61
):

3.1 Definition
.

The
double-crossed product bialgebra
17
17

17

This particular double-crossed product bialgebra is nothing different from the
quantum double

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}),\mathcal{A}_{0};\mathbf{r})
. Remark also that the double-crossed product bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
can be obtained as a twist from the tensor algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}
with
\gamma(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)=\varepsilon(a)\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b,c)\varepsilon(d)
, see e.g.
73
;
58
;
61
.

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
is the vector space
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}
that becomes a bialgebra with coproduct and counit induced from the coalgebra structure of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and multiplication given by
18
18

18

Of course, one should ask whether this definition of the multiplication indeed leads to a well-defined bialgebra. This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, together with the actions given in (
68
), form a so-called
matched pair of bialgebras
. See e.g.
73
;
58
;
61
for details.

(a\otimes b)(c\otimes d)=\sum a(b_{(1)}\triangleright c_{(1)})\otimes(b_{(2)}\triangleleft c_{(2)})d,

(69)

for
a,c\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
b,d\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Under the completion to the Hopf algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\circ}
, the double-crossed product algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\circ}
is also a Hopf algebra with antipode given by
S(a\otimes b)=(1\otimes S(a))(S(b)\otimes 1)
.

Note here that the maps
a\mapsto a\otimes 1
and
b\mapsto 1\otimes b
give the (natural) inclusions of the bialgebras
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
, respectively. Moreover, it can be directly calculated that for the matrix corepresentations
\mathcal{T}(u)
and
\mathcal{T}(0)
in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, the product (
69
) is given by

\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}(0)\right)\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{3}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{4}(0)\right)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{2\underline{3}}(0,v)\left[\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}(u)\mathcal{T}_{\underline{3}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}(0)\mathcal{T}_{4}(0)\right]\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{3}}(0,v),

(70)

where we denote the matrix indices for elements in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
with non-trivial spectral parameter with an underline
\underline{i}
. Note here that the appearance of the
R
-matrix is expected from the exchange in the order of the matrices
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{3}}(v)
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}(0)
, i.e. the double-crossed product (
70
) looks exactly the same as just the product of elements in
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Moreover, the algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
is also cotriangular, with the universal
r
-form given by
73
;
58
;
61

\mathbf{r}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)=\sum\mathbf{r}(a_{(1)},d_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(a_{(2)},c_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(b_{(1)},d_{(2)})\mathbf{r}(b_{(2)},c_{(2)}),

(71)

for
a,c\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
b,d\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Note that the right-hand side is given by the same expression as the factorisation of
\mathbf{r}(ab,cd)
for the
r
-form of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
, signifying again that the double-crossed product exactly resembles the ordinary product on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
.

Of course, one might wonder why we need to consider such a double-crossed product at all if it resembles the same structure as
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. However, we will see that this algebra deforms more naturally into the quantum group that corresponds to the long-range deformation. For example, it can be noted here that the operator

\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{m})1}(u)\coloneq\mathbf{r}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{m}}(0),1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)\right)=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}1}(u)\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},1}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{b_{m},1},

(72)

for
m\geq 1
is exactly the undeformed Lax operator on the increased auxiliary space as given in (
63
). In particular, in the light of Section
2.3
, this Lax operator corresponds to the representation of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{m}}(0)
of
\left(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)^{\mathrm{cop}}
.

The correct long-range deformed FRT-type bialgebra now corresponds to deformations of the double-crossed product bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
. In particular, one introduces a twist to the multiplication. Let us first consider such a twisting for general bialgebras
34
(see also e.g.
73
;
58
;
61
):

3.2 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bialgebra and
\gamma\colon\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
a convolution-invertible bilinear map that is unital, i.e.
\gamma(a,1)=\varepsilon(a)=\gamma(1,a)
. The
twisted
(possibly non-associative) bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is the vector space
\mathcal{A}
with the coalgebra structure induced from
\mathcal{A}
and with twisted multiplication given by

a\cdot_{\gamma}b\coloneq\sum\gamma(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})a_{(2)}\cdot b_{(2)}\gamma^{-1}(a_{(3)},b_{(3)}),

(73)

for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
.

As specified in the definition, the twisted bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is, in general, not associative. However, it is “almost-associative”
73
in the sense that
19
19

19

Such an “almost-associative” bialgebra for which the Drinfeld associator is non-trivial might also be called a
quasibialgebra

73
.

a\cdot_{\gamma}\left(b\cdot_{\gamma}c\right)=\sum\phi\left(a_{(1)},b_{(1)},c_{(1)}\right)\left[\left(a_{(2)}\cdot_{\gamma}b_{(2)}\right)\cdot_{\gamma}c_{(2)}\right]\phi^{-1}\left(a_{(3)},b_{(3)},c_{(3)}\right),

(74)

where
\phi\colon\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is the
Drinfeld associator
given by
34

\phi(a,b,c)\coloneq\sum\gamma\left(b_{(1)},c_{(1)}\right)\gamma\left(a_{(1)},b_{(2)}c_{(2)}\right)\gamma^{-1}\left(a_{(2)}b_{(3)},c_{(3)}\right)\gamma^{-1}\left(a_{(3)},b_{(4)}\right).

(75)

The bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is therefore associative if and only if the Drinfeld associator is trivial, i.e. if it is given by
\phi(a,b,c)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)\varepsilon(c)
, in which case
\gamma
is said to satisfy the
2
-cocycle condition

72
. If
\gamma
is a such a 2-cocyle and
\mathcal{A}
is a Hopf algebra, then
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is also a Hopf algebra with antipode
S^{\gamma}(a)\coloneq\sum f(a_{(1)})S(a_{(2)})f^{-1}(a_{(3)})
where
f(a)\coloneq\sum\gamma(a_{(1)},S(a_{(2)}))
. In the dual picture, as described in Section
2.2
, the twist corresponds to a modification of the coproduct of the corresponding dual quantised universal enveloping algebra, where the additional possibly non-trivial Drinfeld associator turns the Hopf-algebra into a quasi-Hopf algebra
34
. We will discuss this in more detail in Section
3.5
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is coquasitriangular, then
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is also coquasitriangular (in a possibly non-associative sense). In particular, let
\mathbf{r}
be the
r
-form for
\mathcal{A}
, then the
r
-form
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}
for
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is given by
34
(see also e.g.
73
;
58
;
61
)

\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a,b)\coloneq(\gamma_{21}\ast\mathbf{r}\ast\gamma^{-1})(a,b)=\sum\gamma(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathbf{r}\left(a_{(2)},b_{(2)}\right)\gamma^{-1}\left(a_{(3)},b_{(3)}\right),

(76)

such that the braiding property
\sum\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})a_{(2)}\cdot_{\gamma}b_{(2)}=\sum b_{(1)}\cdot_{\gamma}a_{(1)}\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a_{(2)},b_{(2)})
holds for elements in
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
as well. Remark that if
\mathcal{A}
is cotriangular, i.e.
\mathbf{r}^{-1}=\mathbf{r}_{21}
, then
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
is also cotriangular. As noted in Section
2.3
, the factorisation property (
27
) is tightly linked to the associativity of the algebra. Since, after twisting, the algebra is no longer necessarily associative, one also does not expect the factorisation property to hold. Indeed, it can be verified that
34

\begin{split}\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a\cdot_{\gamma}b,c)&=\left(\phi_{312}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{13}\ast\phi^{-1}_{132}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{23}\ast\phi_{123}\right)(a,b,c)\\
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a,b\cdot_{\gamma}c)&=\left(\phi^{-1}_{231}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{13}\ast\phi_{213}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{12}\ast\phi^{-1}_{123}\right)(a,b,c).\end{split}

(77)

Remark here in particular that, if the Drinfeld associator is trivial, and therefore the algebra is associative, one does obtain the ordinary factorisation rule (
27
). It can be noted that the Drinfeld associator, and therefore a possible long-range term, appears in the product rule (
77
) for the
r
-form. In particular, as we will show shortly, this additional Drinfeld associator is exactly what gives rise to the long-range deformations of the Lax operator as given in Section
3.2
. Note that the non-associative factorisation rule also modifies the Yang-Baxter equation. In particular, it follows that the twisted
r
-form satisfies the non-associative Yang-Baxter equation
34

\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{12}\ast\phi_{312}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{13}\ast\phi^{-1}_{132}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{23}\ast\phi_{123}=\phi_{321}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{23}\ast\phi^{-1}_{231}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{13}\ast\phi_{213}\ast\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}_{12}.

(78)

Therefore, for a general twisted bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
, the Yang-Baxter equation (
28
) does not hold. Of course, this then also means that the cotriangular bialgebra does not generate a tower of commuting charges, and therefore does not give rise to an integrable model. However, as we will see, the twisted bialgebras that we will be considering for the long-range deformations will still have some (perturbatively) associative subalgebra, such that the Yang-Baxter equation also still holds (perturbatively).

In order to describe these associative subalgebras, we will introduce some additional notation. In particular, we will introduce the following grading on
\mathcal{A}_{0}
: we define
\mathcal{A}^{(0)}_{0}\coloneq\mathbb{C}
and for
n\geq 1
we define

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}\coloneq\mathrm{span}\left\{t_{i_{1},j_{1}}(0)\cdot\cdots t_{i_{n},j_{n}}(0):1\leq i_{1},...,i_{n}\leq\dim V\text{ and }1\leq j_{1},...,j_{n}\leq\dim V\right\}.

(79)

That is,
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{0}
consists of monomials in
t_{ij}(0)
of degree
n
. As a vector space, the algebra can be decomposed as
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}
. In addition, we consider the subsets of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
with elements that have degree less or degree more than some
n\geq 0
, given respectively by

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\leq n)}\coloneq\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq n)}\coloneq\bigoplus_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}.

(80)

Similarly, we let
\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})
be the non-unital subset of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
generated by the elements
t_{ij}(0)
and
t^{(-k)}_{ij}
for
k\geq 2
and
1\leq i,j\leq N
. Note that this subset is equal to the non-unital
reduced algebra
(see e.g.
70
), such that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})=\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})
as a vector space. The grading can now be used to split the double-crossed product algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
into two parts. In particular, for
m\geq 1
, one can write

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}=\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq m)}\right)\right]\oplus\left[\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\leq m-1)}\right]

(81)

as a vector space, where we identify
\mathcal{A}_{0}\cong\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
on the right side. Note here that the first term in this direct sum is a unital subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
. As we will see, subalgebras of this form for appropriate values of
m
will correspond to the unital subalgebras that remain associative after twisting for the long-range deformations.

3.3.1
Twist for the local deformation

We can now consider the twist of the double-crossed product algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
that corresponds to the local operator long-range deformation. In particular, let
k\geq 2
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
. We consider a twisting function

\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda)\coloneq\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon+\lambda\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\in\mathrm{Hom}\big((\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})\otimes(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}),\mathbb{C}\big)[\![\lambda]\!],

(82)

where we expand
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
around
\lambda=0
up to first order in
\lambda
and the zeroth order term is just the trivial twist, i.e.
\gamma^{(0)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a,b)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)
for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
. We then define the first order correction
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}
to be given by

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})\\
\coloneq&\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}},\end{split}

(83)

for
0\leq N\leq M
, and where we used the notation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}(u_{i})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
. For this particular definition, we set a term
\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}
in the sum to
0
if it “overflows” the element
\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}=0
for
n+k-1>M
. Of course, we should check whether
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is well-defined, i.e. is invariant under the RTT-relation (
19
). Indeed, it can be readily verified that

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{i}\underline{j}}\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{j}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{N}}\otimes b,c\otimes d\right)=\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{j}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{N}}\otimes b,c\otimes d\right)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{i}\underline{j}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{i}\underline{j}}\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes b,\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{j}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{N}}\otimes d\right)=\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes b,\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{j}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{N}}\otimes d\right)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{i}\underline{j}}\end{gathered}

(84)

and

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{P}_{ij}\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{j}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},c\otimes d\right)=\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{j}\mathcal{T}_{i}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},c\otimes d\right)\mathcal{P}_{ij}\\
\mathcal{P}_{ij}\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes b,c\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{j}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}\right)=\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}\left(a\otimes b,c\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{j}\mathcal{T}_{i}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N}\right)\mathcal{P}_{ij},\end{gathered}

(85)

for
a,c\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
b,d\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and we suppressed the explicit dependence on the spectral parameters in (
84
) in our notation, i.e.
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}(u_{i})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
. Note here that the relations (
85
) for the twist (
97
) only hold precisely because the second term in the double-crossed product algebra corresponds to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, i.e. its elements have a trivial spectral parameter, such that the braiding operator is just given by the permutation operator. In particular, a twist of the same form as (
97
) could not have been (consistently) defined on the quantum double
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
. Also note that
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes 1,c\otimes 1)=0
and
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(1\otimes b,1\otimes d)=0
, such that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as subalgebras
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}),\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
remain undeformed.

Importantly, the twisted bialgebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})
is non-associative at first order in
\lambda
. In particular, let us also expand the Drinfeld associator in
\lambda
, i.e. write
\phi_{\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda)=\phi^{(0)}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\lambda\phi^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
with
\phi^{(0)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a,b,c)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)\varepsilon(c)
. It can then be directly calculated that the non-zero terms in the first-order correction to the Drinfeld associator for the elements
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)
,
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}(u_{\mathbf{a}})\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k}(0)
are given by

\begin{split}\phi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k})=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k})\neq 0,\end{split}

(86)

where we used the notation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}(u_{i})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
. It therefore follows from (
78
) that the Yang-Baxter equation does not hold in general. One might be worried whether this will then give rise to an integrable model. Luckily, the twisted bialgebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})
does still have a (large) associative substructure. In particular, as shown in Appendix
A.2
, we have the following result:

3.3 Proposition
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-1)}\right)
, then
\phi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}(a,b,c)=0
.

We can now use this result to construct the Lax operator and
R
-matrix and show that they satisfy the RLL relations, such that the integrability of the corresponding model is ensured. Firstly, we consider the vector
\mathbf{b}\coloneq(b_{1},...,b_{k-1})
and let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{k-1}}(0)
. It can then be directly calculated from (
83
) that

\begin{split}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1})&=0,\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}})&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{1,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{a}}\mathfrak{m}_{1,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{a}}.\end{split}

(87)

In light of (
72
), we define the deformed Lax operator on the increased auxiliary space to be given by

\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-1})1}(u)\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{k-1}}(0),1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)).

(88)

We can now use (
87
) to evaluate this Lax operator. In particular, using the definition of
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}
as given in (
76
), we get

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}=\left(1+\lambda\Big(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{1,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{a}}\mathfrak{m}_{1,b_{1},...,b_{k-1}}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{a}}\Big)\right)\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),\end{split}

(89)

Note here that this Lax operator is exactly equal to the Lax operator obtained from the local operator long-range deformation as given in (
64
)! Alternatively, we could have used the factorisation property (
77
) and the fact that
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}=(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes 1)(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}})
to write

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{m}}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}),\end{split}

(90)

where in the second equality we used that
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{b},1}
(note that
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
remains undeformed under the twisting), such that the second and third Drinfeld associators in (
77
) cancel each other. In particular, it can be seen that the long-range term in the Lax operator is exactly given by the non-vanishing Drinfeld associator as calculated in (
86
). This shows that the long-range nature of this deformation is exactly due to the breaking of the associativity of the double-crossed product bialgebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})
.

It can be noted now that the Lax operator (
88
) corresponds to a representation of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(0)
of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-1)}\right)(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})
. Since it follows from Proposition
3.3
that this subalgebra is associative up to first order in
\lambda
, this also means that there is an
R
-matrix that satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation, and such that the RLL-relation is satisfied. In particular, let also
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},...,d_{k-1})
, then the
R
-matrix is given by

\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0}),\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{0})).

(91)

It now follows immediately from (
78
) and Proposition
3.3
that

\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}(u)\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})1}(v)=\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})1}(v)\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}(u)\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(92)

and

\begin{split}&\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{e}\mathbf{f})}(u,w)\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})(\underline{e}\mathbf{f})}(v,w)\\
=&\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})(\underline{e}\mathbf{f})}(v,w)\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{e}\mathbf{f})}(u,w)\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),\end{split}

(93)

where
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{k-1})
and
u,v,w\in\mathbb{C}
. The above equations now ensure the integrability of the corresponding long-range deformed spin chain up to first order in the deformation parameter.

Lastly, in order for the Lax operator (
88
) to be a proper representation of the algebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})^{\text{cop}}
, it also better be true that a product of elements in the second coordinate factorises. In particular, it can be noted here that
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes(\cdot))
defines a linear map
\mathcal{A}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
, which is just the
\ell
-functional for the twisted double-crossed product bialgebra. In light of Section
2.3
, this function corresponds to an element in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
if it satisfies the correct product factorisation property. Here, it follows immediately from Proposition
3.3
and (
77
) that

\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{2})=\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2})\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(94)

such that indeed also the Lax operator factorises in this way at first order in
\lambda
. In fact, since all the Drinfeld associators corresponding to higher products in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
vanish, the representation of
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(0)
on a spin chain of length
L
factorises into a product of Lax operators, similar to (
36
). Therefore, the monodromy matrix for the long-range models is also just given by a product of Lax operators.

We remark here that it is now not necessarily true that the
R
-matrix factorises into a product of Lax operators, since

\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0}),\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}}(\mathbf{0})\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{0})\right)\neq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0}),1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{c}(\mathbf{0})\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{0})\right).

(95)

Therefore, we see that our Lax operator and
R
-matrix do not have the same properties as described in
45
;
46
. As also noted in Section
3.2
, this is due to the fact that our Lax operators are not regular, i.e. do not reduce to the permutation operator at
u=0
, which is something that was assumed to be true in
45
;
46
. The corresponding twisted
R
-matrices are regular and reduce the permutation operator for
u,v=0
. Note that one could now in principle also use the twisted
R
-matrix as a Lax operator, i.e. define
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{1},2,...,k)}(u,v)\coloneq\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{1},2,..,k)}(u,v)
.
20
20

20

It is, however, not guaranteed that the corresponding integrable model continues to be integrable up to second order in
\lambda
. In fact, since the auxiliary space of the
R
-matrix is required to increase when going to higher-order corrections, such that also the value of
k
increases, it is expected that the integrable model defined through this
R
-matrix as a Lax operator is only
quasi-integrable
.
Since the factorisation property (
95
) does not hold, this then gives, in general, rise to inequivalent charges with respect to the ones obtained from
\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}(u)
. Also, one might wonder whether a Lax operator could alternatively be defined through an
\ell
-functional
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{C}
, i.e. set
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(0),\mathcal{T}_{\underline{1}}(v)\otimes 1)
to be the Lax operator. However, it can be easily verified that the corresponding Drinfeld associators do not vanish, such that the RLL-relations are not satisfied, and therefore no integrable model is obtained in this way.

3.3.2
Twist for the boost deformation

Next, we will discuss the twist corresponding to the long-range deformation that is generated by the boost operator. A lot of its discussion will be similar to Section
3.3.1
, so we will be more brief. For
k\geq 3
, we consider a twisting function

\gamma_{k}(\lambda)\coloneq\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon+\lambda\gamma^{(1)}_{k}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\in\mathrm{Hom}\big((\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})\otimes(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}),\mathbb{C}\big)[\![\lambda]\!],

(96)

for which first-order term is given by

\begin{split}&\gamma_{k}^{(1)}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}\right)\coloneq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(n+1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{n,\underline{\mathbf{b}},(n+1,...,M)}\mathcal{R}_{(n+1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}},\end{split}

(97)

for
0<N<M
, and where we used the notation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}(u_{i})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
, and
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
is the reduced algebraic charge density as given in Definition
2.10
. Similar to the local operator deformation, this twist element satisfies the relations given in (
84
) and (
85
), such that
\gamma^{(1)}_{k}
is invariant under the RTT relations (
19
) and therefore well-defined. Note here again that one could only have such a well-defined twist of this form precisely because the second term in the double-crossed product algebra corresponds to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, i.e. its elements have a trivial spectral parameter, such that the braiding operator is just given by the permutation operator. Also, remark here that
\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes 1,c\otimes 1)=0
and
\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(1\otimes b,1\otimes d)=0
, such that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as subalgebras
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}),\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
remain undeformed.

The twisted algebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k})
is, again, in general non-associative at first order in
\lambda
, where we consider the expansion for the Drinfeld associator
\phi_{k}=\phi_{k}^{(0)}+\lambda\phi_{k}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
with
\phi^{(0)}_{k}(a,b,c)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)\varepsilon(c)
. For example, it can be directly calculated that the non-zero terms in the Drinfeld associator for the elements
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
are given by

\begin{split}\phi^{(1)}_{k}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k-1})=\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k-1})-\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1),\end{split}

(98)

However,
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k})
still has a large associative subalgebra. Namely, as shown in Appendix
A.3
, we have the following result:

3.4 Proposition
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-2)}\right)
, then
\phi_{k}^{(1)}(a,b,c)=0
.

This result can then be used to construct a Lax operator and an
R
-matrix that satisfy the RLL-relations and the Yang-Baxter equation. Firstly, let
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},...,b_{k-2})
and define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{k-2}}(0)
, then it can be calculated that

\gamma^{(1)}_{k}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\right)=0\text{ and }\gamma^{(1)}_{k}\left(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}\right)={}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{b}}.

(99)

In light of (
72
), the deformed Lax operator on the increased auxiliary space is defined to be equal to

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k}}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{k-2})1}(u)&\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{k}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{k-2}}(0),1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)).\end{split}

(100)

We can now use (
99
) and the definition of
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}
as given in (
76
) to obtain

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}^{\gamma_{k}}=\left(1+\lambda\cdot{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{a},\mathbf{b}}\right)\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),\end{split}

(101)

Note here that this Lax operator is exactly equal to the Lax operator obtained from the boost operator long-range deformation as given in (
65
)! Moreover, we could have also written this Lax operator in terms of the Drinfeld associator like in (
90
), where it could then be seen that the long-range term in the Lax operator is exactly given by the non-vanishing Drinfeld associator as calculated in (
98
), which again shows that the long-range nature of this deformation is exactly due to the breaking of the associativity of the double-crossed product bialgebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k})
.

Also here it can now be noted that the Lax operator corresponds to a representation of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(0)
of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-2)}\right)(\gamma_{k})
. Since it follows from Proposition
3.4
that this subalgebra is associative up to first order in
\lambda
, there is also an
R
-matrix such that the RLL relation is satisfied. In particular, let also
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},...,d_{k-1})
, then the corresponding
R
-matrix is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{k}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)&\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{k}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0}),\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{0})\right)\end{split}

(102)

It now follows from Proposition
3.4
that the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation, similar to (
92
) and (
93
), are satisfied for the Lax operator
\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k}}
(
100
) and the above
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{k}}
. Moreover, a similar factorisation property for the Lax operator as given in (
94
) holds for
\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k}}
, such that this Lax operator also defines a proper representation of
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}
in the twisted algebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k})
. In particular, the monodromy matrix for the boost deformation is just given by a product of Lax operators. Similar to (
95
), the
R
-matrix does
not
factorise into a product of Lax operators.

3.3.3
Twist for the bilocal deformation

Lastly, we will discuss the twisting element for the long-range deformation that is generated by the bilocal operators. This discussion will again be very similar to the ones in Sections
3.3.1
and
3.3.2
, such that we will be more brief. For
2\leq k<\ell
, we consider a twisting function

\gamma_{k|\ell}(\lambda)\coloneq\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon+\lambda\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\in\mathrm{Hom}\big((\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})\otimes(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}),\mathbb{C}\big)[\![\lambda]\!],

(103)

for which first-order term is given by

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes 1))\\
&\coloneq\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,N+\ell-2)}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,N+\ell-2),\underline{\mathbf{a}},N+\ell-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,N+\ell-2)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+3-\ell,...,M)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{N+2-\ell,\underline{\mathbf{b}},(N+3-\ell,...M)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+3-\ell,...,M)}\\
&+\sum_{n=N+2,}^{N+\ell-1}\sum_{m=N+2-\ell}^{n-\ell}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,M)}[\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n-k+1,...,n},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,M)}][\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m,...,m+\ell-1},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(1,...,M)}]\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(1,...,M)}\\
&+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,M)}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n+\ell-k,...,n+\ell-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,....M)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(n+1,...,M)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{n,\underline{\mathbf{b}},(n+1,...,M)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(n+1,...,M)}^{-1}\\
&+\sum_{\mathclap{n=N+1}}^{M}\ \mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,n-1)}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,n-1),\underline{\mathbf{a}},n}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(1,...,n-1)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(1,...,M)}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{n-\ell+1,...,n}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(1,...,M)}.\end{split}

(104)

for
0<N<M
, where we used the notation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{\underline{i}}(u_{i})
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneq\mathcal{T}_{i}(0)
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}
are the charge densities as defined in (
54
), and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
and
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}
are the reduced algebraic charge density as given in Definition
2.10
. Note here the ordering in the second entry of the twist element, which we defined in terms of

(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes 1)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(N+1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M}\right)\mathcal{R}_{(N+1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}},

(105)

in order to shorten the notation for the (rather long) twisting element. Also, similar to the twist for the local operator, a term
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n-1+1,...,n}
or
\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m,...,m+\ell-1}
might “overflow” the elements
\mathcal{T}_{1},...,\mathcal{T}_{M}
, in which case we set the overflowing term to 0. For example, we set
\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{m,...,m+\ell-1}=0
if
m+\ell-1>M
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{n-k+1,...,n}=0
if
n-k+1<1
. Also, remark here that, under the substitution
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}\to 1
and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\to 0
, this twisting element reduces to the twist
\gamma_{\ell}^{(1)}
for the boost operator deformation as given in (
97
). Therefore, one might view the boost deformation as a special case of the bilocal deformation.

Similar to the local operator and boost operator deformations, this twist element satisfies the relations given (
84
) and (
85
), such that the twist is invariant under the RTT-relations (
19
) and therefore well-defined. Moreover, the only reason one could define such a twist is that the second term in the double-crossed product algebra corresponds to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and therefore has trivial spectral parameters. Also, remark here that
\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}(a\otimes 1,c\otimes 1)=0
and
\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}(1\otimes b,1\otimes d)=0
, such that the subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}),\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
remain undeformed. We should note here that the representation of the twist element as given in (
104
) is not unique. For example, one can rewrite terms using the Sutherland equations as given in Proposition
2.11
.

Also here, the twisted algebra
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k|\ell})
is in general not associative at first order in
\lambda
, where we similarly consider the expansion for the Drinfeld associator
\phi_{k|\ell}=\phi_{k|\ell}^{(0)}+\lambda\phi_{k|\ell}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
with
\phi^{(0)}_{k|\ell}(a,b,c)=\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)\varepsilon(c)
. For example, it can be directly calculated that the Drinfeld associator for the elements
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
is non-vanishing. However,
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma_{k|\ell})
still has a large associative subalgebra. Namely, as shown in Appendix
A.4
, we have the following result:

3.5 Proposition
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq\ell-1)}\right)
, then
