\phi_{k|\ell}^{(1)}(a,b,c)=0
.

Under the substitution
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}\to 1
and
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}\to 0
, one can then also view Proposition
3.4
for the boost operator deformation as a special case of the above Proposition.

This result can then again be used to construct a Lax operator and an
R
-matrix that satisfy the RLL-relations and the Yang-Baxter equation. Firstly, let
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1})
and define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{\ell-1}}(0)
, then it can be calculated that

\begin{split}\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\right)&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\ {}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{\mathbf{b},\underline{a},1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1},1},\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{k|\ell}\left(1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}\right)&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{b_{\ell-k},...,b_{\ell-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\ {}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{1,\underline{a},\mathbf{b}}.\end{split}

(106)

In light of (
72
), the deformed Lax operator on the increased auxiliary space is then defined to be given by

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}_{(\underline{a},b_{1},...,b_{\ell-2})1}(u)&\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{k}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{\ell-1}}(0),1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}(0)).\end{split}

(107)

We can now use (
106
) and the definition of
\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}
as given in (
76
) to obtain

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}&=\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\\
&+\lambda\left(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(k)}_{b_{\ell-k},...,b_{\ell-1}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}_{1,\underline{a},\mathbf{b}}\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}-\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})1}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{\mathbf{b},\underline{a},1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}_{b_{1},...,b_{\ell-1},1}\right),\end{split}

(108)

Note here that this Lax operator is exactly equal to the Lax operator obtained from the bilocal operator long-range deformation as given in (
66
)! Moreover, we could have also written this Lax operator in terms of the Drinfeld associator like in (
90
), where it could then be seen that the long-range term in the Lax operator is exactly given by the non-vanishing Drinfeld associator.

Similar to before, the Lax operator corresponds to a representation of the matrix corepresentation
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(0)
of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq\ell-2)}\right)(\gamma_{k|\ell})
. Again, it follows from Proposition
3.5
that this subalgebra is associative up to first order in
\lambda
, such that there is an
R
-matrix such that the RLL-relation is satisfied. In particular, let also
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},...,d_{\ell-1})
, then the corresponding
R
-matrix is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)&\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(u)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{0}),\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{0})\right)\end{split}

(109)

It now follows from Proposition
3.5
that the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation, similarly to (
92
) and (
93
), are satisfied for the Lax operator
\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}
(
107
) and the above
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}
. Moreover, a similar factorisation property for the Lax operator as given in (
94
) holds for
\mathscr{L}^{\gamma_{k|\ell}}
, such that this Lax operator also defines a proper representation of
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}}
in the twisted algebra. In particular, the monodromy matrix for the bilocal deformation is just given by a product of Lax operators. Similar to (
95
), the
R
-matrix does
not
factorise into a product of Lax operators.

As remarked in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
, if the spin chain has an additional Lie algebra symmetry, then the bilocal operators involving a Lie algebra generator also provide long-range deformations of the model
13
. This should therefore also correspond to a twist of the corresponding quantum algebra. In order to write down such a twist, one should first derive the Sutherland-type equations for the Lie algebra generators, i.e. bring the commutator
[\Delta(J)_{12},\mathscr{L}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}(12)}]
into the form of a Sutherland equation like in Proposition
2.11
. Then, the twist for the bilocal operator
[\mathbb{Q}_{k}|J]
is simply the same as (
104
), but where the charges
\mathfrak{q}^{(\ell)}
are replaced by the Lie algebra generator
J
, and the reduced algebraic charge densities
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}
are replaced by the terms in the Sutherland equation for
J
. One can then follow the proof for Proposition
3.5
to find that there is also a subalgebra of the deformed quantum algebra for which the corresponding Drinfeld associator vanishes. Since such a Lie algebra symmetry, and therefore the corresponding long-range deformations, are model-dependent, we will not discuss them in more detail.

3.4
Some examples

Up until now, we have described the long-range deformation of the corresponding quantum groups in full generality for any Yang-Baxter integrable spin chain. Let us now give some explicit examples of the Lax operators and
R
-matrices for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
and
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformations of the XXX spin chain. In particular, in this section, we will solely focus on the FRT-bialgebra associated to the
R
-matrix for the Yangian
\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
, which is given by
80

\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)=\frac{u-v-\mathcal{P}_{12}}{u-v-1}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N})[\![u,v]\!],

(110)

where
\mathcal{P}_{12}\colon e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\mapsto e_{j}\otimes e_{i}
is the permutation operator. Since this
R
-matrix is given in terms of this permutation operator, it will follow that all the algebraic charge densities, and therefore the long-range deformations of the Lax operators and
R
-matrices, are also given in terms of the permutation operator. In particular, using the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{12}^{-1}(u,v)=\mathcal{R}_{21}(v,u)
, it can be readily calculated that the second algebraic charge density acting on two sites is given by

\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}(u,v)\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{12}-1}{(u-v)^{2}-1}.

(111)

Note here that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(0,0)=1-\mathcal{P}_{12}
, which is indeed the Hamiltonian density for the XXX spin chain. It now also readily follows that the third algebraic charge density acting on two sites is given by

\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{12}(u,v)=\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)=-\frac{2(u-v)(\mathcal{P}_{12}-1)}{\left((u-v)^{2}-1\right)^{2}},

(112)

where it can be noted that
\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{12}(0,0)=0
, which is a direct consequence of the
R
-matrix being of difference form. Any higher algebraic charge density acting on two sites can be calculated by simply taking additional derivatives, i.e.
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}(u,v)=\frac{d^{k-2}}{du^{k-2}}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)
.

Next, let us compute the higher coproducts of the algebraic charge densities. Recall here that the coproducts for the
R
-matrix on three sites are given by

\mathcal{R}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u,w)\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(12)3}(u,v;w)=\mathcal{R}_{13}(u,w)\mathcal{R}_{23}(v,w).

(113)

Using (
43
), we now find that the coproduct for the second algebraic charge density acting on three sites is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{R}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}(u,v)\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{13}(u,w)\mathcal{R}_{12}(u,v)+\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)\\
&=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{12}-1}{(u-v)^{2}-1}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{13}-1}{(u-w)^{2}-1}+\frac{(u-v)[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]+\mathcal{P}_{13}-\mathcal{P}_{23}}{((u-v)^{2}-1)((u-w)^{2}-1)}.\end{split}

(114)

Here, it can be noted that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(0;0,0)=1-\mathcal{P}_{12}+1-\mathcal{P}_{23}
, which is the sum over the two Hamiltonian densities of the XXX spin chain. The coproduct for the third algebraic charge density acting on three sites can now be directly calculated to be given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)&=\frac{d}{du}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)\\
&=-\frac{2(u-v)(\mathcal{P}_{12}-1)}{((u-v)^{2}-1)^{2}}-\frac{2(u-w)(\mathcal{P}_{13}-1)}{((u-w)^{2}-1)^{2}}+\frac{[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]}{((u-v)^{2}-1)((u-w)^{2}-1)}\\
&\quad-2\left(\frac{u-v}{(u-v)^{2}-1}+\frac{u-w}{(u-w)^{2}-1}\right)\frac{(u-v)[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]+\mathcal{P}_{13}-\mathcal{P}_{23}}{((u-v)^{2}-1)((u-w)^{2}-1)}.\end{split}

(115)

Remark that
\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(23)}(0;0,0)-\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{23}(0,0)=[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]
, which is indeed equal to the third charge of the XXX spin chain. Any higher algebraic charge densities acting on three sites can now be calculated by taking higher derivatives, i.e.
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)=\frac{d^{k-2}}{du^{k-2}}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}(u;v,w)
. Similarly, higher coproducts of the algebraic charge densities can be calculated by taking derivatives of higher coproducts of the
R
-matrix. Also, all of these calculations can be easily performed using
Mathematica
for any arbitrary
R
-matrix.

We can now compute the Lax operator and the
R
-matrix for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
generated long-range deformation of the XXX spin chain up to first order in the deformation parameter. First, let us consider the reduced algebraic charge density with a non-trivial spectral parameter in the second coordinate, which is given by

\begin{split}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1,\underline{2},3}(0,u,0)&=\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(\underline{2}3)}(0;u,0)-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{2}}(0,u)\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{13}(0,0)\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{2}}(0,u)\\
&=\frac{2u}{(-1+u^{2})^{2}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{12}+\mathcal{P}_{23}-\mathcal{P}_{13}-1\right)+\frac{1+u^{2}}{(-1+u^{2})^{2}}[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}],\end{split}

(116)

where we used the notation that an underline signifies a non-trivial insertion of a spectral parameter. Note here that
1,\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{23},\mathcal{P}_{13}
and
[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]
form a basis for the
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
invariant matrices in
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
21
21

21

Recall that the Schur-Weyl dual of
\mathrm{SL}_{N}
acting on
(\mathbb{C}^{N})^{\otimes k}
is the symmetric group
S_{k}
, such that indeed all the
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
-invariant matrices are given in terms of permutation operators.
such that the above form is the unique expression in terms of these basis elements. From (
100
), it then follows that the Lax operator for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation is given by

\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)3}(u)\coloneq\left(1+\lambda\cdot{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{3,\underline{1},2}(0,u,0)\right)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}3}(u,0)\mathcal{P}_{23}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(117)

which is just a product of the matrices that we gave in (
110
) and (
116
).
22
22

22

We remark that this Lax operator differs from the one found in e.g.
68
. One has, in principle, some freedom in defining the Lax operator, as one can, for example, perform a similarity transformation on the auxiliary space and still obtain the same charges. Moreover, our Lax operator is not regular, as it also describes the deformation of the first charge
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
, which was not included in
68
. However, one could construct a regular Lax operator by a reparametrisation
u\to u-\frac{\lambda}{u}
, by imposing a non-trivial spectral parameter
\frac{\lambda}{u}
in the second coordinate of the auxiliary space, and by then removing the singularity in
u
through a similarity transformation on the auxiliary space. That is, it can be verified that the Lax operator

\mathscr{L}^{\text{reg}}_{(\underline{1}2)3}(u)\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{1}2)3}^{\lambda}\left(u-\frac{\lambda}{u}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{u}\frac{d}{dv}\bigg|_{v=0}\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)3}(u,v;0)-\frac{\lambda}{u}[\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{\underline{1}2}(u,0),\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)3}(u,0;0)]+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})

(118)

is regular. Moreover, it can be derived that this Lax operator provides the proper range-three Hamiltonian correction (i.e. without the
\mathbb{Q}_{4}
term) and also has an associated
R
-matrix such that the RLL relations are satisfied. We remark that this regularised Lax operator is less natural to consider. Moreover, it is unknown whether such a regularisation can be done for arbitrary boost and bilocal deformations.
Similarly, we can use (
102
) to obtain the expression for the
R
-matrix. In particular, note here that the double coproduct for the undeformed
R
-matrix is given by

\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}4}(u,0)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{13}}(u,v)\mathcal{P}_{24}\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{3}}(0,v).

(119)

From (
102
), it then follows that the deformed
R
-matrix for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)&\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\\
&\quad+\lambda\left({}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{4,\underline{1},2}(0,u,0)\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)-\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v){}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{2,\underline{3},4}(0,v,0)\right),\end{split}

(120)

where we can recognise the usual formula for a twisted
R
-matrix. It now follows from Proposition
3.4
that the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation for this Lax operator and
R
-matrix are satisfied. In particular, we have

\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)5}(u)\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{3}4)5}(v)=\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{3}4)5}(v)\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)5}(u)\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(121)

which can also be explicitly checked in, for example,
Mathematica
. This shows that we get an integrable model up to first order in
\lambda
. That is, if one considers the monodromy matrix
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}b)}(u)\coloneq\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}b)L}(u)\cdots\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}b)1}(u)

and the corresponding transfer matrix
t^{\lambda}(u)\coloneq\mathrm{tr}_{\underline{a}b}\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}_{\underline{a}b}(u)
, then it follows from (
121
) that
[t^{\lambda}(u),t^{\lambda}(v)]=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
, such that the transfer matrix generates a tower of commuting charges up to second order in the deformation parameter. In particular, it can now be directly calculated (using, for example,
Mathematica
) that the second charge for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathbb{Q}_{2}(\lambda)=t^{\lambda}(0)^{-1}\frac{d}{du}t^{\lambda}(u)\Big|_{u=0}&=\sum_{n=1}^{L}(1-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1})+\lambda\Big[(1-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+2})-4(1-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1})\Big]\\
&\quad+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{Q}_{4}(0)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}).\end{split}

(122)

On the right, one can recognise the two-loop correction to the dilatation operator in the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
sector of planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
9
;
7
.
23
23

23

Remark that the explicit form in terms of permutation operators is different from
9
;
7
, as the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
invariant expression in terms of products of permutation operators is not unique. However, one can explicitly calculate that our correction to the Hamiltonian is indeed equal to the two-loop correction to the dilatation operator as given in
9
;
7
.
The additional
\mathbb{Q}_{4}
charge can be simply removed by a change of basis of the charges, or via the reparametrisation
u\to u-\frac{\lambda}{u}
of the spectral parameter.
24
24

24

Remark that this reparametrisation introduces a pole at
u=0
. In particular, the charges cannot be defined by evaluation at
u=0
of the derivatives in (

). Rather, the charges should be (equivalently) defined via contour integrals around
u=0
.
We have therefore explicitly constructed the Lax operator and the
R
-matrix for planar
\mathcal{N}=4
up to two-loop.

Next, let us consider the long-range deformation corresponding to the bilocal
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
operator. We will denote the charge densities by
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(2)}_{12}=1-\mathcal{P}_{12}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{123}=[\mathcal{P}_{12},\mathcal{P}_{13}]
. Moreover, as remarked in Section
2.5
, since the
R
-matrix is of difference form, we have
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)=\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}(u,v)
and
{}^{r}\tilde{Q}^{(2)}_{1,\underline{2},3}(0,u,0)=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{2}3}(u,0)
by (
48
). Recall that the undeformed Lax operator on a tripled auxiliary space is given by
\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{1}23)4}(u)\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}4}(u,0)\mathcal{P}_{24}\mathcal{P}_{34}
. From (
107
), and also using the property for the reduced algebraic charge density as given in (
48
), we find that the Lax operator for the
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}23)4}(u)\coloneq\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{1}23)4}(u)+&\lambda\Big(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(23)}(u;0,0)\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(2)}_{23}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(23)}(u;0,0){}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{4,\underline{1},2}(0,u,0)\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{1}23)4}(u)\\
&-\mathscr{L}_{(\underline{1}23)4}(u)\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(23)}(u;0,0)\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{1}4}(u,0)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(23)}(u;0,0)\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{234}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}).\end{split}

(123)

Similarly, we can use (
109
) to get the
R
-matrix for the bilocal deformation. Recall here that the undeformed
R
-matrix on a tripled auxiliary space is given by

\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)=\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}6}(u,0)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}5}(u,0)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{14}}(u,v)\mathcal{P}_{26}\mathcal{P}_{25}\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{4}}(0,v)\mathcal{P}_{36}\mathcal{P}_{35}\mathcal{R}_{3\underline{4}}(0,v).

(124)

Before writing down the deformation of this
R
-matrix, let us first write down the explicit twist element for the deformation. From (
104
), it follows that the twist for the
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
bilocal deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)&\coloneq\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(235)}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{1}6}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(235)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(356)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{2,\underline{4},3}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}3}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(2356)}[\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(2)}_{56},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(2356)}][\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{235},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(2356)}]\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(23)}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(2356)}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(2)}_{35}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(2356)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(356)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{2,\underline{4},3}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}3}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(2356)}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}^{(2)}_{56}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(2356)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(56)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{3,\underline{4},5}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(23)}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{1}5}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(23)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(2356)}\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{235}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(23)}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(56)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{1}(235)}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{1}6}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(235)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(2356)}\mathfrak{q}^{(3)}_{356}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{4}(23)},\end{split}

(125)

where we left out the explicit dependence on the spectral parameters on the right side. However, one should note that the coordinate
\underline{1}
corresponds to an insertion of the spectral parameter
u
, while
\underline{4}
corresponds to the spectral parameter
v
. In particular, we used the notation
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}(i_{1},...,i_{n})}\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1},i_{n}}(u,0)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1},i_{1}}(u,0)
,
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4}(i_{1},...,i_{n})}\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4},i_{n}}(v,0)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{\underline{4},i_{1}}(v,0)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{\underline{1},i}\coloneq\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{\underline{1},i}(u,0)
and
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{i,\underline{4},j}\coloneq{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{i,\underline{4},j}(0,v,0)
. From (
109
), it then follows that the
R
-matrix for the
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)&\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\\
&\quad+\lambda\left(\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{(\underline{4}56)(\underline{1}23)}(v,u)\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)-\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{(\underline{1}23)(\underline{4}56)}(u,v)\right).\end{split}

(126)

Similar to before, it follows from Proposition
3.5
that the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation for the above Lax operator and
R
-matrix for the
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation are satisfied, which can also be explicitly verified using, for example,
Mathematica
. In particular, this implies that the transfer matrix
t^{\lambda}(u)\coloneq\mathrm{tr}_{\underline{a}b_{1}b_{2}}\big(\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}b_{1}b_{2})L}\cdots\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}b_{1}b_{2})1}\big)
generates a tower of commuting charges up to first order in
\lambda
, since
[t^{\lambda}(u),t^{\lambda}(v)]=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
. For example, it can be calculated in
Mathematica
that the correction to the second charge for the bilocal
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformation of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
XXX Heisenberg spin chain is given by

\begin{split}\mathbb{Q}_{2}(\lambda)=\sum_{n=1}^{L}&(1-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1})+\lambda\Big[4(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1}-1)+(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+3}-1)-(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+2}-1)\\
&+(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1,n+3}-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+3,n+1})-2(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1}\mathcal{P}_{n+2,n+3}-1)\\
&+(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+2,n+3,n+1}-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+2,n+1,n+3})+(\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1,n+3,n+2}-\mathcal{P}_{n,n+1,n+2,n+3})\Big]\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),\end{split}

(127)

where we used the notation
\mathcal{P}_{n_{1},n_{2},...,n_{k}}\coloneq\mathcal{P}_{n_{1},n_{k}}\mathcal{P}_{n_{1},n_{k-1}}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
, which simply corresponds to the shift operator. It can be seen that the second charge gets a range-four correction at first order in
\lambda
under the
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
deformation. This term is also expected to give a contribution to the dilatation operator in the four-loop correction of planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
9
;
7
. Of course, one can use the results of our paper to also compute the Lax operators,
R
-matrices and charges for general boost and bilocal deformations and for general FRT-bialgebras up to first order in
\lambda
.

3.5
Long-range deformed Yangian

Up until now, we have described the long-range deformations in terms of the FRT-bialgebras. As described in Section
2.2
, these FRT-bialgebras are dual to the quantised universal enveloping algebra constructions. In particular, we can now also describe the long-range deformations of these quantised universal enveloping algebras. For the purpose of this section, we will focus on the Yangian algebras, as it is the easiest example to consider. We will mostly follow the same steps as in Section
3.3
, but now applied to the bialgebra dual. We remark that the following discussion is expected to generalise naturally to more general quantum affine algebras.

In order to describe the long-range deformations of the Yangian
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
(see Section
2.2
), we first need to consider a similar “doubling” of the quantum group, as was needed for the FRT-bialgebra. Let us first discuss the dual to the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Let
(\rho_{0},V)
be a fundamental representation of
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, such that
\rho_{0}(x)=\tilde{x}\in\mathrm{End}(V)
and
\rho_{0}(\mathcal{J}(x))=0
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
, where
\tilde{x}
denotes the fundamental representation of
x
. Next, we consider the linear map
\Phi_{0}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
given by
\Phi_{0}(x)(a)\coloneq\langle a,x\rangle
for
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the bilinear map (
24
) for the representation
\rho_{0}
and restricted to
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho_{0}})
. Remark that, by the properties of the dual pairing (
25
),
\Phi_{0}
is automatically a bialgebra homomorphism.
25
25

25

One should remember here that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
is not actually a bialgebra, since the image of the “coproduct” of
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{0}
does not necessarily lie in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
(see footnote
5
). However, since
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
is a bialgebra, this also ensures that
\mathrm{Im}(\Phi_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
is a bialgebra. Therefore,
\Phi_{0}
, when restricting the codomain to its image, is a bialgebra homomorphism.
We then define the bialgebra
26
26

26

If
S(\ker(\Phi_{0}))\subseteq\ker(\Phi_{0})
, then
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\cong\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})/\ker(\Phi_{0})
is a Hopf algebra, with the antipode for
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
induced from
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. Although this is believed to be true, we do not provide a proof for this.

\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\coloneq\mathrm{Im}(\Phi_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*},

(128)

which one can think of as being the algebra of Yangian operators acting on the “evaluation zero spin chains”. Here, we should explain our notation for
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, which refers to
\hbar=\infty
. Recall that the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
corresponds to all matrix elements with spectral parameter
u=0
. For the Yangian algebra, the spectral parameters have “units of
\hbar
”. For example, one can reintroduce
\hbar
in the
R
-matrix (
110
) for the Yangian by replacing
u\to u/\hbar
. In this sense, the
u\to 0
limit corresponds to the
\hbar\to\infty
limit. Similarly, since
\rho_{0}(\mathcal{J}(x))=0
, it can be noted from (
21
) that
(\rho_{0}\otimes\rho_{0})(\Delta(\mathcal{J}(x)))=\frac{\hbar}{2}(\rho_{0}\otimes\rho_{0})([x\otimes 1,t])
, i.e. only the term proportional to
\hbar
survives for the coproduct. This coproduct structure can be emulated in (
21
) by “taking
\hbar
to be large”. In practice, this
\hbar\to\infty
interpretation corresponds to the fact that neither
\mathcal{A}_{0}
nor
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
has an appropriate classical limit. Indeed,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is the free algebra generated by
t_{ij}(0)
, with an
R
-matrix given by the permutation operator
\mathcal{P}
. There is no limit in which
\mathcal{P}
can be approximated around the identity, in which the elements
t_{ij}(0)
become commutative. In that sense, one can also view
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
as the “pure quantum” limit of the Yangian algebra, which cannot be approximated around a classical universal enveloping algebra. We emphasise here that
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
is defined through (
128
), and is not obtained by literally taking the
\hbar\to\infty
limit of the Yangian algebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
.

We can now consider the doubling of the Yangian algebra with this
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, which is the dual description to Definition
3.1
. In particular, we would like to construct a double-crossed
co
product algebra between
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, which is obtained by twisting the coproduct structure of the tensor product bialgebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
by the universal
R
-matrix. Here, however, there are some difficulties, as the universal
R
-matrix for the Yangian is only defined in a topological completion. Therefore, the coproduct for the double-crossed coproduct algebra can also only be defined in such a completion. For the Yangian
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, we define the
u
-deformed coproduct

\Delta_{u,v}(x)\coloneq(\mathcal{B}_{u}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{v})(\Delta(x))\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(u)\!)\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(v)\!)

(129)

for
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, which satisfies
\mathcal{R}(u-v)\Delta_{u,v}(x)=\Delta_{v,u}^{\mathrm{cop}}(x)\mathcal{R}(u-v)
, and reduces to the ordinary coproduct in the limit
u,v\to 0
. See e.g.
50
;
51
for more details on such a
u
-deformed coproduct structure. Next, we will introduce a similar notation as before for the FRT-bialgebras, to distinguish between elements in
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
. In particular, we define

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{12}}(u-v)&\coloneq\mathcal{R}(u-v)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(u)\!)\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(v)\!),\\
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{1}2}(u)&\coloneq(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Phi_{0})(\mathcal{R}(u))\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(u)\!)\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\infty},\\
\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{2}}(-v)&\coloneq(\Phi_{0}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R}(-v))\in\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})(\!(v)\!),\\
\mathcal{R}_{12}(0)&\coloneq(\Phi_{0}\otimes\Phi_{0})(\mathcal{R}(0))\in\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\infty},\end{split}

(130)

where it should be remarked that, since
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
corresponds to evaluation zero representations, the element
(\Phi_{0}\otimes\Phi_{0})(\mathcal{R}(0))
is well-defined in
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, and is in fact also the
R
-matrix for
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
. An underline in the coordinate signifies an element in
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, while no underline corresponds to an element in
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
.

We can now define the double-crossed coproduct bialgebra for the topological completion:

3.6 Definition
.

The
u
-deformed double-crossed coproduct bialgebra

\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
is the algebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, with counit induced from the tensor product bialgebra, and with a
u
-deformed coproduct given by

\Delta_{u,v}(x\otimes y)\coloneq\sum\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{3}}^{-1}(-v)\left[\mathcal{B}_{u}(x_{(1)})\otimes y_{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{v}(x_{(2)})\otimes y_{(2)}\right]\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{3}}(-v),

(131)

for
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
.

In this definition, one can recognise the dual construction of (
69
). That is, there is a dual pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})\times\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\right)\to\mathbb{C},

(132)

given by
\langle a\otimes b,x\otimes y\rangle=\langle a,x\rangle y(b)
for
a\otimes b\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
x\otimes y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, and
\langle a,x\rangle
is the dual pairing (
24
) between
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. For the FRT-bialgebra, we saw in (
69
) that the double-crossed product construction changes the product structure. Correspondingly, for the Yangian, it’s the coproduct structure that gets altered. We emphasise, however, that
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
is not a bialgebra in the strict sense, as the coproduct is only well-defined in the appropriate topological completion.
27
27

27

It might be true that the
u,v\to 0
limit of the coproduct
\Delta_{u,v}
for the double-crossed coproduct bialgebra is actually well-defined, just as is the case for the coproduct (
129
) of the (single) Yangian
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, such that
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
becomes a proper bialgebra. However, it is currently unknown whether this is indeed the case.
The double-crossed coproduct algebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
is pseudo-quasitriangular, with the universal
R
-matrix given by

\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\coloneq\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{4\underline{1}}(-u)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{13}}(u-v)\mathcal{R}_{24}(0)\mathcal{R}_{2\underline{3}}(-v),

(133)

such that
\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\Delta_{u,v}(x\otimes y)=\Delta_{v,u}^{\text{cop}}(x\otimes y)\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)
for
x\otimes y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
. Remark that this universal
R
-matrix is dual to the
r
-form as given in (
71
).

Next, we can consider twists of this double-crossed coproduct bialgebra. Here, we should now twist the
u
-deformed coproduct. In particular, we consider an invertible element
28
28

28

Remark that this twisting element is fully written in terms of algebraic charge densities, which correspond to derivations of the universal
R
-matrix and are well-defined algebra elements of the Yangian as discussed in Section
2.5
.

\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\in\big[(\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty})(\!(u)\!)\otimes(\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty})(\!(v)\!)\big][\![\lambda]\!],

(134)

which is the twisting element dual to the twisting function for the boost or bilocal deformation as given in (
97
) and (
104
), such that

\lim_{u,v\to 0}\langle(a\otimes b)\otimes(c\otimes d),\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\rangle=\gamma(a\otimes b,c\otimes d),

(135)

for
a\otimes b,c\otimes d\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R}_{\rho})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
, where it should be noted that the
u,v\to 0
limit is well-defined under the dual pairing, as also the
R
-matrix in a given representation is well-defined at zero spectral parameter. We then have the following definition:

3.7 Definition
.

The
cotwisted
u
-deformed quasi-bialgebra

(\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty})(\mathcal{F})
is the algebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, with a counit induced from
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, and with a cotwisted
u
-deformed coproduct given by

\Delta^{\mathcal{F}}_{u,v}(x\otimes y)\coloneq\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\Delta_{u,v}(x\otimes y)\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v),

(136)

for
x\otimes y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
.

In general, this cotwisted bialgebra is a quasi-bialgebra, i.e. the coproduct is non-coassociative (in the
u
-deformed sense).
29
29

29

Also here, it is unknown whether the cotwisted coproduct
\Delta^{\mathcal{F}}_{u,v}
has a well-defined
u,v\to 0
limit, which would make this
u
-deformed quasi-bialgebra into a proper quasi-bialgebra.
However, Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
imply that, after some number of successive applications of the coproduct, the corresponding Drinfeld coassociator becomes trivial up to first order in
\lambda
, such that the cotwisted algebra still has a (perturbatively) coassociative substructure. Moreover, the cotwisted algebra is pseudo-quasitriangular, with the cotwisted
R
-matrix given by

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{F}}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\coloneq\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{3}4)(\underline{1}2)}(v,u)\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v)\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{(\underline{1}2)(\underline{3}4)}(u,v),

(137)

which is the dual of the twisted
r
-form as given in (
76
).

It is important to note that, for the Yangian (or rather its double-crossed coproduct bialgebra
\mathcal{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\hat{\bowtie}_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
), it is the
co
algebra structure that is deformed upon twisting. In particular, the algebra relations for the Yangian remain untouched.
30
30

30

This also implies that one cannot simply obtain the long-range deformed Yangian from a
RTT-realisation
with the deformed
R
-matrix. Namely, the RTT relations define the algebra structure on the Yangian, which remains undeformed under the cotwisting. In this sense, the
R
-matrix that defines these algebra relations should also remain undeformed. The RTT relation involving the deformed
R
-matrix, therefore, only applies to the long-range deformed dual Yangian, i.e. the FRT-bialgebra. Of course, the
\ell
-functional
\bm{\ell}(a)\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a,\cdot)
does still define a bialgebra homomorphism between the (co-opposite) deformed FRT-bialgebra and the deformed Yangian algebra, such that in this way one may still obtain Yangian elements from the RTT realisation.
This is also expected for the long-range integrable models, which were shown to still have an underlying Yangian symmetry on the doubly-infinite spin chains
12
;
4
;
5
. The twist of the coproduct structure merely has the consequence that the representation of the Yangian on the spin chain of length
L
gets deformed. Remark here that, for specific representations, the
u,v\to 0
limits of the
u
-deformed coproducts will become well-defined, as the
R
-matrix in this limit has a well-defined representation, such that, in these representations, the coproduct can just be considered as an ordinary coproduct for a bialgebra.

Remember that the
\ell
-functionals as described in Section
2.3
define representations for the FRT-bialgebra via the representations of the Yangian, and that these then define the Lax operator. For the long-range deformation, we have a similar story: the long-range deformed Lax operator corresponds to the map

\bm{\ell}^{\gamma}:(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\text{cop}}(\gamma)\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty},\quad\bm{\ell}^{\gamma}(a\otimes b)(t_{ij}(0))\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(a\otimes b,1\otimes t_{ij}(0)),

(138)

where
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty}
is the appropriate completion of
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{*}
such that
\bm{\ell}^{\gamma}(a\otimes b)\in\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty}
for
a\otimes b\in(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\text{cop}}(\gamma)
. In particular, the representation for the FRT-bialgebra corresponding to the Lax operator is determined by the representation of
\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
, which corresponds to the algebra of Yangian operators acting on the zero evaluation spin chains. In this way, we see that the long-range deformation of the spin chain is explicitly obtained through the deformation of the representation of the underlying Yangian algebra due to the twisting of the coproduct.

4
Discussion and outlook

In this paper, we have explicitly constructed the FRT-bialgebras relevant for the long-range deformations of homogeneous Yang-Baxter integrable spin chains up to first order in the deformation parameter
\lambda
. In Section
2.5
, we introduced new algebra elements, the algebraic charge densities, whose algebraic properties allowed us to give a conjecture for the explicit expression of the charge densities, and to derive the generalised Sutherland equations in Section
2.6
. These Sutherland equations were then used in Section
3.2
to derive the Lax operators for the full family of long-range deformations up to first order in the deformation parameter. From the form of these Lax operators, it was observed that the appropriate FRT-bialgebra corresponding to the long-range deformation is given by the double-crossed product bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
in order to describe the increased auxiliary space that is needed to describe the wrapping interactions. Moreover, we then gave the explicit twisting elements in Section
3.3
corresponding to the local, boost and bilocal long-range deformations. In particular, it was observed here that the twisted FRT-bialgebras were in general non-associative, and that the corresponding non-trivial Drinfeld associator encodes the long-range interaction terms of the Lax operator. The main results of this paper were then given in Propositions
3.3
,
3.4
and
3.5
, in which it is shown that the Drinfeld associator vanishes for a large subalgebra of the deformed FRT-bialgebra. This implies that the algebra still has a large associative substructure for which the Yang-Baxter equation is satisfied. In particular, this gave rise to explicit expressions for the Lax operators and
R
-matrices for the long-range deformations, which manifestly satisfy the RLL-relation and the Yang-Baxter equation. In Section
3.4
, we gave explicit examples of these Lax operators and
R
-matrices for the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
and
[\mathbb{Q}_{2}|\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformations of the XXX Heisenberg spin chain. Lastly, in Section
3.5
, we also derived the long-range deformation for the Yangian algebra. Here, we saw that the coproduct for the deformed algebra can only be defined in a topological completion. However, this topologically completed coproduct still possesses a pseudo-triangular universal
R
-matrix that braids the coproduct, and that corresponds to the
R
-matrix for the long-range deformed models under a given representation.

There are several interesting future research directions one can take from here:

•

Higher-order corrections.
We have now only described the quantum group deformations up to first order in the deformation parameter
\lambda
. The next obvious step would be to describe the deformed quantum groups up to all orders in
\lambda
, which are still expected to correspond to twists of the double-crossed product algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Moreover, it is then expected that the long-range nature of the perturbative integrable spin chain is encoded in the perturbatively associative structure of this deformed algebra. In particular, for every
n\geq 1
, it is expected that there is a subalgebra of
(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0})(\gamma)
such that
\phi^{(m)}
vanishes for all
m\leq n
in that subalgebra. In this way, the algebra would give rise to perturbative Lax operators and
R
-matrices which satisfy the RLL-relations and the Yang-Baxter equations up to order
\lambda^{n}
. We remark here that, although the Lax operators and
R
-matrices could then only be defined perturbatively, the corresponding quantum group could possibly be defined non-perturbatively, thus giving an exact quantum group structure. The family of long-range deformations defined by the local, boost and bilocal operators would then provide a moduli space of such long-range deformed quantum groups.

•

Classification.
We have now derived the twisted FRT-bialgebras by starting from the deformation equation described in
4
;
5
. A natural question would be whether this family of long-range deformations does indeed give the full classification of long-range deformations, and therefore also the full set of “long-range twists” that one could perform on the FRT-bialgebra. It is currently unknown whether this family describes all the long-range deformations or whether more twists exist that give rise to a perturbatively associative substructure of the quantum algebra.

•

Algebraic Bethe Ansatz.
One of the motivations for understanding the quantum group structure of the long-range deformations is that it is expected to provide insights into the long-range Algebraic Bethe Ansatz. Indeed, our construction now allows us to, at the very least, identify the correct
N
-magnon creation operators. In particular, it can be remarked that, for a given twist
\gamma
, we can define a Lax operator with non-trivial multiple spectral parameters in the auxiliary space. In particular, for
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{N})
, we can define

\mathscr{L}^{\gamma}_{(\underline{\mathbf{a}}\mathbf{b})1}(u_{\mathbf{a}})\coloneq\mathscr{L}^{\gamma}_{(\underline{a}_{1},...,\underline{a}_{N},\mathbf{b})1}(u_{1},...,u_{N})=\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}_{1}}(u_{1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}_{N}}(u_{N})\otimes\mathcal{T}_{b_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{b_{k-2}},1\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}).

(139)

In particular, when for example restricting to
6
-vertex models, such a Lax operator provides a representation for elements of the form
B(u_{1})\cdots B(u_{N})\otimes t_{i_{1}i_{1}}(0)\cdots t_{i_{m},i_{m}}(0)
on the spin chain, where
B(u)\coloneq t_{12}(u)\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})[\![u]\!]
. Note here that these elements are manifestly symmetric in the rapidities
u_{i}
and, when acting on the vacuum state
|\Omega\rangle\coloneq|{\downarrow}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|{\downarrow}\rangle
, give states with
N
flipped up-spins. Therefore, linear combinations of such operators provide a good candidate for the
N
-magnon creation operators that must appear in the long-range Algebraic Bethe Ansatz. In fact, in Appendix
B
in (
178
), we indeed find that creation operators of this form correspond to the
N
-magnon states for the
B[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
-deformed XXX spin chain. Namely, in Appendix
B
we are able to map these
N
-magnon states to the
N
-magnon states that were found in
48
using the
\Theta
-morphism. Of course, we have not given a full proof that these are the
N
-magnon states, nor have we provided a proper Algebraic Bethe Ansatz that gives the eigenenergies and the Bethe equations for the long-range models. However, one should note that it follows from Proposition
3.3
,
3.4
or
3.5
that, for
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},...,c_{N})
, there is an
R
-matrix

\mathcal{R}^{\gamma}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{d})}(v,u_{\mathbf{c}})\coloneq\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}})=\mathbf{r}^{\gamma}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{a}}(v)\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{b}},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}_{1}}(u_{1})\cdots\mathcal{T}_{\underline{c}_{N}}(u_{N})\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{d}})

(140)

that gives the braiding between the ordinary Lax operator
\mathscr{L}(v)
and the Lax operator with multiple spectral parameters
\mathscr{L}(u_{1},...,u_{N})
as given in (
139
). It is then expected that this braiding relation allows one to “commute” the creation operators of the form
B(u_{1})\cdots B(u_{N})\otimes t_{i_{1}i_{1}}(0)\cdots t_{i_{m},i_{m}}(0)
through the transfer matrix constituents to obtain a “wanted” and an “unwanted” term, thus providing a proper Algebraic Bethe Ansatz.

•

Reflection equation.
We have now provided a consistent method of getting Lax operators and
R
-matrices that correspond to the long-range deformation. However, this only gives deformations on the closed and (possibly quasi-) periodic spin chain. In order to describe open spin chains, one should additionally find solutions to the reflection equation for the twisted
R
-matrix
23
;
88
;
63
. Remark that if
K_{\underline{a}}(u)
is a solution to the reflection equation
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{ab}}(u,v)K_{\underline{a}}(u)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{ba}}(v,-u)K_{\underline{b}}(v)=K_{\underline{b}}(v)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{ab}}(u,-v)K_{\underline{a}}(u)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{ba}}(-v,-u)
, then
K_{\underline{a}}(u)
is also a solution to the reflection equation for the (undeformed)
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)
on the increased auxiliary space. Importantly, after twisting the
R
-matrix, one finds that
K^{\mathcal{F}}(u)
is a solution to the twisted reflection equation if (not necessarily only if) it satisfies

\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(u,v)K_{\underline{a}}(u)=K^{\mathcal{F}}_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}(u)\mathcal{F}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}(-u,v).

(141)

For general twists, it is unknown whether there is such a matrix
K^{\mathcal{F}}(u)
for any given
K(u)
. However, remark that the twists for the local and boost operators as given in (
83
) and (
97
), respectively, act trivially on the coordinate
a
. In particular, this implies that
K_{\underline{a}\mathbf{b}}^{\mathcal{F}}(u)=K_{\underline{a}}(u)
gives a solution to (
141
), such that
K(u)
is also a solution to the twisted reflection equation for the local and boost deformation. One might use this solution to the reflection equation to study the long-range deformations on an open spin chain for the local and boost operators
10
. For the deformation corresponding to the bilocal operators, however, it is currently unknown what the solution to the reflection equation would be.

•

Proving the conjecture.
As a side result, we found a conjecture for the explicit expression of the charge densities in terms of the algebraic charge densities. A possible future research direction would therefore be to prove Conjecture
2.7
. Here, it is expected that the description for the higher-order corrections to the deformation of the quantum group requires additional identities for the algebraic charge densities. These additional identities might already be enough to prove the statement.

•

AdS/CFT.
The second charge in the
\mathcal{B}[\mathbb{Q}_{3}]
long-range deformation of the XXX Heisenberg spin chain appears as the two-loop dilatation operator for the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
sector of planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. We therefore also provide a quantum group theoretical description for this subsector up to two-loop. By the AdS/CFT correspondence, one might also expect this same quantum group structure to appear in the (classical) string theory. However, it is currently unknown how to make the connection to the corresponding (super) Yangian algebra on the string theory side
17
;
11
. Moreover, it is known that, in the comparison between the gauge and string theories, one should be careful with the order of limits
60
;
86
. In particular, the thermodynamic limit and the expansion in the coupling constant do not commute. Interestingly, a similar phenomenon is observed on the quantum group level. In particular, as discussed in Section
3.2
, the deformed monodromy matrix on the doubly-infinite spin chain is given by
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{\underline{a},(...)}=\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}+\lambda[\mathcal{X}(0),\mathscr{L}_{\underline{a},(...)}]+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
. Remark that, since the first-order term is given by a commutator, this monodromy matrix satisfies the RLL relation with the original undeformed
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}(u,v)
. Alternatively, long-range deformations on finite spin chains are described by the deformed Lax operators
\mathscr{L}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})n}
for which there is a deformed
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}
. We therefore immediately see two different descriptions for the long-range deformation on the doubly-infinite spin chain, depending on the order of the thermodynamic limit and the deformation. Namely, by first doing the thermodynamic limit and then the deformation, one obtains a monodromy matrix of which the corresponding
R
-matrix is given by the undeformed
\mathcal{R}(u,v)
. If one first performs the deformation and then takes the thermodynamic limit, one obtains a monodromy matrix of which the corresponding
R
-matrix is given by the deformed
\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{(\underline{a}\mathbf{b})(\underline{c}\mathbf{d})}
. It is expected that the second description is the correct one for the correspondence with string theory, as it is this description that also includes the wrapping corrections.

Appendix A
Proofs

In this appendix, we will provide all the proofs for the statements made in the main text in Sections
2.5
and
3.3
.

A.1
Proofs for the algebraic charge densities

We will first consider the proofs of the various propositions and lemmas that are given in Section
2.5
. Firstly, we consider the proof for Proposition
2.6
:

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

First note that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}+\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{(12)3}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}+\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{23}
, such that the statement is true for
k=2
. The higher-order charges are then defined by
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}=\mathcal{D}_{1}^{k-2}(\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{12}=\mathcal{D}_{2}^{k-2}(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{12})
for
k\geq 3
. Therefore, we can calculate the coproducts of the higher-order charges by repeated application of the derivations on
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(23)}
. Note here that

\mathcal{D}_{1}\left(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}\right)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(k+1)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}+[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}],

and similarly

\mathcal{D}_{3}\left(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}\right)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k+1)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}+[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(2)}_{23},\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}].

By repeated application of the derivation, this then immediately shows that the coproducts
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(23)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(12)3}
must be of the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(23)}&=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{12}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=k-1}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right],\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(12)3}&=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{23}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=k-1}\tilde{c}^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{23},\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-2}})}_{23},\left[\cdots,\left[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{23}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})},\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{23}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{13}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}\mathcal{R}_{23}\right]\cdots\right]\right]\right],\end{split}

where
\overline{\ell_{i}}=\ell_{i}+1
and
\ell_{i}\geq 1
for some coefficients
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\tilde{c}^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. These coefficients must now satisfy some recurrence relations. Firstly, note here that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{D}_{1}\left(\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right]\right)\\
&=\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{1})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right]\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\cdots\left[\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n}})}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{12}\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{i}}+1)}_{12}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{n-1}})}_{12}\right].\end{split}

Using this relation, it can be seen that the coefficient
c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}
can be obtained from summing over all coefficients
c^{k-2}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{i}-1,...,\ell_{n}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
if
\ell_{1}\neq 1
. If
\ell_{1}=1
, then there is an additional contribution to the coefficient
c^{k-1}_{1,\ell_{2},\ell_{3},...,\ell_{n}}
, which comes from the factor corresponding to the coefficient
c^{k-2}_{\ell_{2},\ell_{3},...,\ell_{n}}
after taking the derivation in
\mathcal{Q}^{(k-1)}_{1(23)}
or
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k-1)}_{(12)3}
. This then shows that the coefficients must satisfy the recurrence relation

c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{i}-1,...,\ell_{n}}.

(142)

where
c^{k}_{k}=1
,
c^{k}_{0,\ell_{2},...,l_{n}}=c^{k}_{\ell_{2},...,\ell_{n}}
and
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}=0
if
\ell_{i}=0
for some
2\leq i\leq n
. It can be easily seen that the same recurrence relation holds for the coefficients
\tilde{c}^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}
, such that
\tilde{c}^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}=c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}
.

We will now show that this recurrence relation is solved by

c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}=\prod_{m=2}^{n}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{i}-1\choose\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\ell_{i}},

(143)

for
n\geq 2
and
\ell_{i}\geq 1
for
2\leq i\leq n
. Remember here that
c^{k}_{k}=1
. Firstly, since
{a\choose 0}=1
for any
a\in\mathbb{N}
, it can be directly noted that
c^{k}_{0,\ell_{2}...,\ell_{n}}=c^{k}_{\ell_{2},...,\ell_{n}}
. Moreover, if there is some
2\leq m\leq n
such that
\ell_{m}=0
, then (
143
) contains a term

{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{i}-1\choose\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\ell_{i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\ell_{i}-1\choose\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\ell_{i}}=0,

(144)

since the top coefficient is now smaller than the bottom coefficient. Therefore, we also have
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}=0
if
\ell_{m}=0
for some
2\leq m\leq n
. It can be noted that
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}={k-1\choose k-\ell_{n}}c^{k-\ell_{n}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n-1}}
. Moreover, recall that the binomial coefficients satisfy Pascal’s identity

{n\choose k}={n-1\choose k-1}+{n-1\choose k}.

(145)

we will now prove that (
143
) solves the recurrence relation (
142
) using induction. Firstly, for
n=2
, we have
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}={k-1\choose\ell_{1}}
, with
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=k
, such that it follows immediately from (
145
) that
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}=c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1}-1,\ell_{2}}+c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}-1}
. Next, let
n\geq 2
, and assume that the recurrence relation holds for all
m\leq n
. We then have

\begin{split}c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}&={k-1\choose k-\ell_{n}}c^{k-\ell_{n}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n-1}}=\left[{(k-1)-1\choose(k-1)-\ell_{n}}+{(k-1)-1\choose(k-1)-(\ell_{n}-1)}\right]c^{k-\ell_{n}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n-1}}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{(k-1)-1\choose(k-1)-\ell_{n}}c^{(k-1)-\ell_{n}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{i}-1,...,\ell_{n-1}}+{(k-1)-1\choose(k-1)-(\ell_{n}-1)}c^{(k-1)-(\ell_{n}-1)}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n-1}}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{i}-1,...,\ell_{n}}.\end{split}

Therefore, by induction, the coefficients
c^{k}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}}
as given in (
143
) indeed solve the recurrence relation (
142
), which then proves the proposition.
∎

Next, we give a proof of Lemma
2.9
:

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

We will prove the Lemma using induction and only for
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
, as the case for
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
is completely analogous. First, for
k=3
, it follows from (
45
) that

\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}=[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}]+\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}.

From (
43
), it follows that
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}=\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathcal{R}_{12}=\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{12}+\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{2\underline{\mathbf{b}}}
, where we used that
\mathcal{R}_{12}=\mathcal{P}_{12}
for evaluation-zero representations. Since
[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{2\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}]=0
, it follows immediately that

\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(2\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}}2)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}.

Now, let
k>3
and assume that

\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,n-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{1(2,...,n-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,n-1)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{1(2,...,n-1)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},

for all
3\leq n<k
. From Proposition
2.6
it follows that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}=\\
&\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}+\sum_{m=2}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{m}=k-1}c^{k-1}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m}}\left[\left[\cdots\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right],\cdots\right],\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m-1}})}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right].\end{split}

Note here that
\overline{\ell_{m}}<k
in the sum on the right side. Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}&=\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1)}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{12}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(3,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(3,...,k-1)}\right)\mathcal{R}_{12}\\
&=\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1)}+\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{2(3,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{2(3,...,k-1)}\right),\end{split}

where was again used that
\mathcal{R}_{12}=\mathcal{P}_{12}
. Note here that the last term in the above equation commutes with
\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}
and with
\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{2}})}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}
. In particular, this gives

\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right]=\left[\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{m}})}_{1(2,...,k-1)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\mathcal{Q}^{(\overline{\ell_{1}})}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right].

It now immediately follows that also

\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k-1,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,k-1)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,k-1)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}.

The result for
\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
of the Lemma now follows from induction. The result for
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
is completely analogous and follows mutatis mutandis.
∎

Next, we will consider an additional lemma for the reduced algebraic charge densities that will be used in the proofs for the long-range deformation of the FRT-bialgebra in Section
3.3
.

A.1 Lemma
.

For
k\geq 2
and
n\geq k-1
, we have

\begin{split}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}},(2,...,n)}&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{b}},(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}},\\
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,n-1),\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}},n}&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,n-1)\underline{\mathbf{a}}}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,n-2),\underline{\mathbf{b}},n}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,n-1)\underline{\mathbf{a}}}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}n}{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}_{(1,...,n-1),\underline{\mathbf{a}},n}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}n}.\end{split}

Proof.

Let
k\geq 2
and
n\geq k-1
. It then follows from the definition of the reduced algebraic charge density
{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
as given in Definition
2.10
and Lemma
2.9
that

\begin{split}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}},(2,...,n)}&=\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}},2,...,n)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,n,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\\
&=R^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,n)}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,n,\underline{\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,....,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}\\
&\quad-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}})}\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{a}},2,...,n)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,....,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right)\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}\\
&\quad+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(\underline{\mathbf{b}},2,...,n)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\right)\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{a}},(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(2,...,n)}+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{1,\underline{\mathbf{b}},(2,...,n)}\mathcal{R}_{1\underline{\mathbf{a}}}.\end{split}

The derivation for the reduced algebraic charge density
{}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(k)}
is completely analogous, thus proving the statement.
∎

Lastly, we will also explicitly write down the proof for Corollary
2.12
:

Proof for Corollary
2.12
.

The proof follows immediately from Proposition
2.11
. In particular, one has

\begin{split}\left[\mathbb{Q}^{(k)},\left(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{am}(u)\right)\right]&=\mathrm{tr}_{a}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}[\mathfrak{q}^{(k)}_{n,...,n+k-1},\overleftarrow{\prod}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{L}_{am}(u)]\right)\\
&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{tr}_{a}\bigg(\mathscr{L}_{a(n+1,...)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{(n,...,n+k-3),a,n+k-2}\mathscr{L}_{a(...,n)}\\
&\qquad\qquad-\mathscr{L}_{a(n+2,...)}{}^{r}\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}_{(n,...,n+k-2),a,n+k-1}\mathscr{L}_{a(...,n+1)}\bigg)=0,\end{split}

where the sum vanishes due to it being a telescoping sum over
\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

A.2
Proofs for the local deformation

This section will be devoted to proving Proposition
3.3
. Before we can prove this proposition, we will first consider the following intermediate result:

A.2 Lemma
.

Let
k\geq 2
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{End}(V^{\otimes k})
, then the twist element
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}
as defined in (
83
) satisfies

\begin{split}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b\cdot b^{\prime},c\otimes d)&=\varepsilon(b)\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b^{\prime},c\otimes d)\\
&\quad+\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b^{\prime}_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c_{(2)}\otimes b^{\prime}_{(2)}\cdot d)\mathbf{r}(b^{\prime}_{(3)},c_{(3)}),\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\cdot c^{\prime}\otimes d)&=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},c^{\prime}\otimes d)\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\\
&\quad+\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(c^{\prime}_{(1)},d_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d_{(2)})\mathbf{r}(c^{\prime}_{(2)},d_{(3)}),\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d\cdot d^{\prime})&=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)\varepsilon(d^{\prime})\end{split}

for all
a,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
b,b,d,d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
such that
c\otimes d\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-1)}\right)
.

Proof.

For the first equality, let
0<N<M<K
, then from the definition of
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}
as given in (
83
), it follows that

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{M+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K})\\
&=\sum_{n=N+1}^{M}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(M+1,...,K)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(M+1,...,K)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(M+1,...,K)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(M+1,...,K)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{M+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K})\\
&\quad+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(N+1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K})\mathcal{R}_{(N+1,...,M)\underline{\mathbf{b}}},\end{split}

(146)

where we used that
[\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1},\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}]=0
for
n\geq N+1
in the second equality. This then proves the first equality.

For the second equality, let
N>0
and
M>N+k-2
, then

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(\underline{\mathbf{b}}\underline{\mathbf{c}})(N+1,...,M)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(\underline{\mathbf{b}}\underline{\mathbf{c}})(N+1,...,M)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)(\underline{\mathbf{b}}\underline{\mathbf{c}})}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)(\underline{\mathbf{b}}\underline{\mathbf{c}})}\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\bigg[\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{c}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{c}}}\bigg]\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)}\bigg[\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\bigg]\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)}\\
&=\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&\quad+\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}(N+1,...,M)},\end{split}

(147)

where in the second equality, it can be seen that the first term in the sum cancels the fourth term in the sum. Note here that the equality would still hold for
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\to 1
for any
M>N
, thus proving the second equality.

For the last equality, let
N>0
,
M>N+k-2
and
K>M
, then we have

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K})\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,K)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,K)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(N+1,...,M)}-\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1}\mathcal{R}_{(1,...,N)\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\\
&=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M})\varepsilon(\mathcal{T}_{M+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K}),\end{split}

(148)

where in the second equality we used that
n+k-1\leq N+k-1<M+1
, such that
[\mathfrak{m}_{n,...,n+k-1},\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}(M+1,...,K)}]=0
. Also, remark that the statement is trivially true under the substitution
\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\to 1
and for any
0<N<M<K
, thus proving the last equality and therefore the statement.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Let
a\otimes b,c\otimes d,e\otimes f\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-1)}\right)
, then

\begin{split}\phi^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d,e\otimes f)&=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(c\otimes d,e\otimes f)\varepsilon(a)\varepsilon(b)+\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,(c\otimes d)(e\otimes f))\\
&\quad-\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}((a\otimes b)(c\otimes d),e\otimes f)-\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)\varepsilon(e)\varepsilon(f).\end{split}

(149)

From Lemma
A.2
, it now follows that

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,(c\otimes d)(e\otimes f))=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(d_{(1)},e_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\cdot e_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}\cdot f)\mathbf{r}(d_{(3)},e_{(3)})\\
&=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(d_{(1)},e_{(1)})\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},e_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}f)\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\mathbf{r}(d_{(3)},e_{(3)})\\
&\quad+\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(e_{(1)},f_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d\cdot f_{(2)})\mathbf{r}(e_{(2)},f_{(3)})\\
&=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(d_{(1)},e_{(1)})\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},e_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}f)\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\mathbf{r}(d_{(3)},e_{(3)})\\
&\quad+\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)\varepsilon(e)\varepsilon(f),\end{split}

(150)

where in the second equality we used that
\sum\mathbf{r}(e_{(1)},d_{(1)})\mathbf{r}(d_{(2)},e_{(2)})=\varepsilon(e)\varepsilon(d)
since
\mathbf{r}
is cotriangular. Similarly, we have

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}((a\otimes b)(c\otimes d),e\otimes f)=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)}\cdot d,e\otimes f)\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\\
&=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\mathbf{r}^{-1}(d_{(1)},e_{(1)})\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},e_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}\cdot f)\mathbf{r}(d_{(3)},e_{(3)})\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\\
&\quad+\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1)}(a\cdot c\otimes d,e\otimes f)\varepsilon(b).\end{split}

(151)

By combining all the terms, and using the fact that
\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\cdot c\otimes d,e\otimes f)=\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(c\otimes d,e\otimes f)\varepsilon(a)
, we find that indeed the Drinfeld associator vanishes, i.e.
\phi^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{m}}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d,e\otimes f)=0
, thus proving the proposition.
∎

A.3
Proofs for the boost deformation

This section will be devoted to proving Proposition
3.4
. In order to prove this proposition, we will use the following intermediate result:

A.3 Lemma
.

Let
k\geq 3
, then the twist element
\gamma_{k}^{(1)}
as defined in (
97
) satisfies

\begin{split}\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b\cdot b^{\prime},c\otimes d)&=\varepsilon(b)\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b^{\prime},c\otimes d)\\
&\quad+\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b^{\prime}_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b,c_{(2)}\otimes b^{\prime}_{(2)}\cdot d)\mathbf{r}(b^{\prime}_{(3)},c_{(3)}),\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b,c\cdot c^{\prime}\otimes d)&=\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\cdot c_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},c^{\prime}\otimes d)\mathbf{r}(b_{(3)},c_{(3)})\\
&\quad+\sum\mathbf{r}^{-1}(c^{\prime}_{(1)},d_{(1)})\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d_{(2)})\mathbf{r}(c^{\prime}_{(2)},d_{(3)}),\\
\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d\cdot d^{\prime})&=\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(a\otimes b,c\otimes d)\varepsilon(d^{\prime})\end{split}

for all
a,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{R})
and
b,b,d,d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
such that
c\otimes d\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathcal{A}^{(\geq 1)}(\mathcal{R})\bowtie_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\geq k-2)}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
0<N<M<K
. From the definition of the twist
\gamma_{k}^{(1)}
as given in (
97
), it then follows that

\begin{split}&\gamma^{(1)}_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{M},\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\mathbf{b}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{M+1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{K})\\
